Semiclassical limits and geometric optics Suppose $V^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x})=V_{0}(\mathbf{x})+W^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x})$ in ( 7.7 ), and we set $\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x},t)=\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x},t)}\exp\left(\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x},t)\right),$ (7.17) where $\rho^{\varepsilon}=|\psi^{\varepsilon}|^{2}$ and $S^{\varepsilon}$ are the density and phase of the wave function, respectively.
Inserting ( 7.17 ) into the GPE ( 7.7 ) and
separating real and imaginary parts give [ 104 , 103 , 28 , 69 , 192 ] $\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}+{\rm div}\,(\rho^{\varepsilon}\;\nabla S^{\varepsilon})+\Omega\hat{L}_{z}\rho^{\varepsilon}=0,$ (7.18) $\displaystyle S_{t}^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla S^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\rho^{\varepsilon}+V^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x})+\Omega\hat{L}_{z}S^{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}}\ \Delta\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}},$ (7.19) where $\hat{L}_{z}=(x\partial_{y}-y\partial_{x})$ .
The equation ( 7.18 ) is the transport equation for the
atom density and ( 7.19 ) the Hamilton-Jacobi equation for
the phase. By formally passing to the limit $\varepsilon\to 0$ (cf. [ 102 ] ), we obtain the system $\displaystyle\rho^{0}_{t}+{\rm div}\,(\rho^{0}\;\nabla S^{0})+\Omega\hat{L}_{z}\rho^{0}=0,$ (7.20) $\displaystyle S^{0}_{t}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla S^{0}|^{2}+\rho^{0}+V_{0}(\mathbf{x})+\Omega\hat{L}_{z}S^{0}=0.$ (7.21) It is well known that this limit process is only correct in the
defocusing case $\beta>0$ before caustic onset, i.e. in
time-intervals where the solution of the Hamilton-Jacobian equation
( 7.19 ) coupled with the atom-number conservation equation
( 7.18 ) is smooth. After the breakdown of regularity,
oscillations occur, which make the term $\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}}\ \Delta\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}$ at least $O(1)$ such that the validity of the formal limit process is destroyed.
The limiting behavior after caustics
onset is not understood yet except in 1D case
without confinement, see [ 122 ] . Also, the focusing case $\beta<0$ is not fully understood yet. Furthermore, by defining the
current densities $\displaystyle{\bf J}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x},t)=\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla S^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\,{\rm Im}\left[\overline{\psi^{\varepsilon}}\nabla\psi^{\varepsilon}\right],$ (7.22) we can rewrite ( 7.18 )-( 7.19 ) as a
coupled Euler system with third-order dispersion terms [ 104 , 103 , 28 , 69 , 192 ] $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}+{\rm div}{\bf J}^{\varepsilon}+\Omega\widehat{L}_{z}\rho^{\varepsilon}=0,$ (7.23) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\bf J}^{\varepsilon}+{\rm div}\left(\frac{{\bf J}^{\varepsilon}\otimes{\bf J}^{\varepsilon}}{\rho^{\varepsilon}}\right)+\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla V^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\left(\rho^{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+\Omega\widehat{L}_{z}{\bf J}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\nabla\left(\rho^{\varepsilon}\nabla^{2}\ln\rho^{\varepsilon}\right).\hskip 9.24994pt$ (7.24) Letting $\varepsilon\to 0^{+}$ in ( 7.23 )-( 7.24 ), formally we get an Euler
system coupling through the pressures [ 104 , 103 , 28 , 69 , 192 ] $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho^{0}+{\rm div}{\bf J}^{0}+\Omega\widehat{L}_{z}\rho^{0}=0,$ (7.25) $\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\bf J}^{0}+{\rm div}\left(\frac{{\bf J}^{0}\otimes{\bf J}^{0}}{\rho^{0}}\right)+\rho^{0}\nabla V_{0}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\left(\rho^{0}\right)^{2}+\Omega\widehat{L}_{z}{\bf J}^{0}=0,\qquad\quad$ (7.26) where ${\bf J}^{0}(\mathbf{x},t)=\rho^{0}\nabla S^{0}$ . The system
( 7.25 )-( 7.26 ) is a coupled isotropic Euler system
with quadratic pressure-density constitutive relations in the
rotational frame. The formal asymptotics is supposed to hold up to
caustic onset time [ 103 , 104 ] . 8. Mathematical theory and numerical methods for dipolar BEC In the last several years, there has been a quest for realizing a
novel kind of quantum gases with the dipolar interaction, acting
between particles having a permanent magnetic or electric dipole
moment. In 2005, the first dipolar BEC
with 52 Cr atoms was successfully realized in experiments at the
Stuttgart University [ 107 ] .
Later in 2011, a dipolar BEC with 164 Dy atoms, whose
dipole-dipole interaction is much stronger than that of 52 Cr,
was achieved in experiments at the
Stanford University [ 138 ] . Very recently in 2012, a dipolar BEC of 168 Er atoms
has been produced in Innsbruck University [ 8 ] .
These successes of
experiments have renewed interests in theoretically studying dipolar BECs. 8.1. GPE with dipole-dipole interaction At temperature $T$ much smaller than the critical temperature $T_{c}$ ,
a dipolar BEC is well described by the macroscopic wave function $\psi=\psi(\mathbf{x},t)$ whose evolution is governed by the
3D Gross-Pitaevskii equation (GPE) [ 187 , 161 , 49 ] $\displaystyle i\hbar\partial_{t}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)=\left[-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+g|\psi|^{2}+\left(V_{\rm dip}\ast|\psi|^{2}\right)\right]\psi,\quad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\ t>0,$ (8.1) where $\mathbf{x}=(x,y,z)^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}^{3}}$ is
the Cartesian coordinate and a harmonic trap potential $V(\mathbf{x})$ is considered here. $g=\frac{4\pi\hbar^{2}a_{s}}{m}$ describes local (or short-range)
interaction between dipoles in the condensate with $a_{s}$ the $s$ -wave scattering length. The long-range dipolar
interaction potential between two dipoles is given by $\displaystyle V_{\rm dip}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\mu_{0}\mu_{\rm dip}^{2}}{4\pi}\,\frac{1-3(\mathbf{x}\cdot\bf n)^{2}/|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{|\mathbf{x}|^{3}}=\frac{\mu_{0}\mu_{\rm dip}^{2}}{4\pi}\,\frac{1-3\cos^{2}(\theta)}{|\mathbf{x}|^{3}},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},$ (8.2) where $\mu_{0}$ is the vacuum magnetic permeability, $\mu_{\rm dip}$ is permanent magnetic dipole moment (e.g. $\mu_{\rm dip}=6\mu_{{}_{B}}$ for 52 C r with $\mu_{{}_{B}}$ being the
Bohr magneton), ${\bf n}=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}$ is the
dipole axis (or dipole moment) which is a given unit vector, i.e. $|{\bf n}|=\sqrt{n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{3}}=1$ , and $\theta$ is the angle
between the dipole axis ${\bf n}$ and the vector $\mathbf{x}$ . The wave
function is normalized according to $\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{2}^{2}:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|\psi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}\;d\mathbf{x}=N,$ (8.3) where $N$ is the total number of dipolar particles in the dipolar BEC. By introducing the dimensionless variables, $t\to\frac{t}{\omega_{0}}$ with $\omega_{0}=\min\{\omega_{x},\omega_{y},\omega_{z}\}$ , $\mathbf{x}\to x_{s}\mathbf{x}$ with $x_{s}=\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{m\omega_{0}}}$ , $\psi\to\frac{\sqrt{N}\psi}{x_{s}^{3/2}}$ ,
we obtain the dimensionless GPE in 3D from ( 8.1 )
as : $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+\beta|\psi|^{2}+\lambda\left(U_{\rm dip}\ast|\psi|^{2}\right)\right]\psi,\quad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\;t>0,$ (8.4) where $\beta=\frac{Ng}{\hbar\omega_{0}x_{s}^{3}}=\frac{4\pi a_{s}N}{x_{s}}$ , $\lambda=\frac{mN\mu_{0}\mu_{\rm dip}^{2}}{3\hbar^{2}x_{s}}$ , $V(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{2}(\gamma_{x}^{2}x^{2}+\gamma_{y}^{2}y^{2}+\gamma_{z}^{2}z^{2})$ is the dimensionless harmonic trapping potential with $\gamma_{x}=\frac{\omega_{x}}{\omega_{0}}$ , $\gamma_{y}=\frac{\omega_{y}}{\omega_{0}}$ and $\gamma_{z}=\frac{\omega_{z}}{\omega_{0}}$ , and the dimensionless long-range
dipolar interaction potential $U_{\rm dip}(\mathbf{x})$ is given as $\displaystyle U_{\rm dip}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{3}{4\pi}\,\frac{1-3(\mathbf{x}\cdot\bf n)^{2}/|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{|\mathbf{x}|^{3}}=\frac{3}{4\pi}\,\frac{1-3\cos^{2}(\theta)}{|\mathbf{x}|^{3}},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.$ (8.5) Although the kernel $U_{\rm dip}$ is highly singular near the origin,
the convolution is well-defined for $\rho\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$ with $U_{\rm dip}*\rho\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$ for $p\in(1,\infty)$ [ 70 ] . Denote the
differential operators $\partial_{{\bf n}}={\bf n}\cdot\nabla$ and $\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}=\partial_{{\bf n}}\partial_{{\bf n}}$ , and notice the
identity [ 23 ] $\displaystyle U_{\rm dip}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{3}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}|^{3}}\left(1-\frac{3(\mathbf{x}\cdot{\bf n})^{2}}{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\right)=-\delta({\bf r})-3\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}\left(\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}|}\right),\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},$ (8.6) we can
re-formulate the GPE ( 8.4 ) as the following
Gross-Pitaevskii-Poisson system (GPPS) [ 23 , 18 , 65 ] $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+(\beta-\lambda)|\psi|^{2}-3\lambda\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}\varphi\right]\psi,\quad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\quad t>0,$ (8.7) $\displaystyle\qquad\nabla^{2}\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)=-|\psi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\qquad\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty}\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)=0,\qquad t\geq 0.$ (8.8) The above GPPS in 3D conserves the mass , or the normalization condition, $\displaystyle N(\psi(\cdot,t)):=\|\psi(\cdot,t)\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|\psi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}\;d\mathbf{x}\equiv\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|\psi(\mathbf{x},0)|^{2}\;d\mathbf{x}=1,\quad t\geq 0,$ (8.9) and energy per particle with $\varphi=\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}|}*|\psi|^{2}$ , $\displaystyle E_{\rm 3D}(\psi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left[\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\psi|^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})|\psi|^{2}+\frac{\beta-\lambda}{2}|\psi|^{4}+\frac{3\lambda}{2}\left|\partial_{{\bf n}}\nabla\varphi\right|^{2}\right]\,d\mathbf{x}.$ (8.10) From
( 8.6 ), it is straightforward to get the Fourier
transform of $U_{\rm dip}(\mathbf{x})$ as $\displaystyle\widehat{(U_{\rm dip})}(\xi)=-1+\frac{3\left({\bf n}\cdot\xi\right)^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}},\qquad\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.$ (8.11) 8.2. Dimension reduction In many physical experiments of dipolar BECs, the condensates
are confined with strong harmonic trap in one or two axis directions,
resulting in a disk- or cigar-shaped dipolar BEC, respectively.
Mathematically speaking, this corresponds to the anisotropic potentials $V(\mathbf{x})$ of the form: Case I (disk-shaped), potential is strongly confined in
vertical $z$ direction
with $\displaystyle V(\mathbf{x})=V_{2}(x,y)+\frac{z^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{4}},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},$ (8.12) Case II (cigar-shaped), potential is strongly confined in
horizontal $\mathbf{x}_{\perp}=(x,y)^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}$ plane with $\displaystyle V(\mathbf{x})=V_{1}(z)+\frac{x^{2}+y^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{4}},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},$ (8.13) where $0<\varepsilon\ll 1$ ( $\varepsilon=1/\gamma_{z}$ in Case I and $\varepsilon=1/\gamma_{r}$ with $\gamma_{x}=\gamma_{y}=\gamma_{r}$ in Case II) is a small parameter describing
the strength of confinement. In such cases, the above GPPS in 3D can be formally reduced
to 2D and 1D, respectively [ 65 ] . In Case I , when $\varepsilon\to 0^{+}$ , evolution of the solution $\psi(\mathbf{x},t)$ of GPPS ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) in $z$ -direction
would essentially occur in the ground state mode of $-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{zz}+\frac{z^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{4}}$ , which is spanned by $w_{\varepsilon}(z)=\varepsilon^{-1/2}\pi^{-1/4}e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}}$ [ 65 , 18 ] . By taking the ansatz $\displaystyle\psi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z,t)=e^{-it/2\varepsilon^{2}}\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t)w_{\varepsilon}(z),\qquad(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z)^{T}=(x,y,z)^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\quad t\geq 0,$ (8.14) the 3D GPPS
( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) can be formally reduced to a quasi-2D equation I [ 65 , 18 ] : $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\phi=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V_{2}+\beta_{2D}|\phi|^{2}-\frac{3\lambda}{2}(\partial_{{\bf n}_{\perp}{\bf n}_{\perp}}-n_{3}^{2}\nabla^{2})\varphi^{2D}\right]\phi,$ (8.15) where $\beta_{2D}=\frac{\beta-\lambda+3\lambda n_{3}^{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\varepsilon}$ , ${\bf n}_{\perp}=(n_{1},n_{2})^{T}$ , $\partial_{{\bf n}_{\perp}}={\bf n}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla={\bf n}_{\perp}\cdot(\partial_{x},\partial_{y})^{T}$ , $\partial_{{\bf n}_{\perp}{\bf n}_{\perp}}=\partial_{{\bf n}_{\perp}}(\partial_{{\bf n}_{\perp}})$ , $\nabla^{2}=\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy}$ and $\displaystyle\varphi^{2D}(x,y,t)=U^{2D}_{\varepsilon}*|\phi|^{2},\quad U^{2D}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\pi^{3/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{-s^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}s^{2}}}\,ds.$ (8.16) In addition, as $\varepsilon\to 0^{+}$ , $\varphi^{2D}$ can be approximated by $\varphi^{2D}_{\infty}$ [ 65 ] as : $\displaystyle\varphi^{2D}_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t)=U_{\rm dip}^{2D}*|\phi|^{2},\quad\hbox{with}\quad U_{\rm dip}^{2D}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})=\frac{1}{2\pi|\mathbf{x}_{\perp}|},$ (8.17) which can be re-written as a fractional
Poisson equation $\displaystyle(-\nabla^{2})^{1/2}\varphi^{2D}_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp}t)=|\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t)|^{2},\quad\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}_{\perp}|\to\infty}\varphi^{2D}_{\infty}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t)=0,\qquad t\geq 0.$ (8.18) Thus an alternative quasi-2D equation II can be obtained as: $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\phi=\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V_{2}+\beta_{2D}|\phi|^{2}-\frac{3\lambda}{2}(\partial_{{\bf n}_{\perp}{\bf n}_{\perp}}-n_{3}^{2}\nabla^{2})(-\nabla^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}(|\phi|^{2})\right)\phi.$ (8.19) Similarly, in Case II , evolution of the solution $\psi(x,y,z,t)$ of GPPS ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) in $(x,y)$ plane
would essentially occur in the ground state mode of $-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy})+\frac{x^{2}+y^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{4}}$ , which
is spanned by $w_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\varepsilon^{-1}\pi^{-1/2}e^{-\frac{x^{2}+y^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}}$ [ 18 , 65 ] . Again, by taking the ansatz $\displaystyle\psi(x,y,z,t)=e^{-it/\varepsilon^{2}}\phi(z,t)w_{\varepsilon}(x,y),\quad t\geq 0,$ (8.20) the 3D GPPS
( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) can be formally reduced to a quasi-1D equation : $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\phi=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{zz}+V_{1}+\beta_{1D}|\phi|^{2}-\frac{3\lambda(3n_{3}^{2}-1)}{8\sqrt{2\pi}\,\varepsilon}\partial_{zz}\varphi^{1D}\right]\phi,\quad z\in{\mathbb{R}},\ t>0,$ (8.21) where $\beta_{1D}=\frac{\beta+\frac{1}{2}\lambda(1-3n_{3}^{2})}{2\pi\varepsilon^{2}}$ and $\displaystyle\varphi^{1D}(z,t)=U_{\varepsilon}^{1D}*|\phi|^{2},\qquad U^{1D}_{\varepsilon}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{2}e^{z^{2}/2\varepsilon^{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\varepsilon}\int_{|z|}^{\infty}e^{-s^{2}/2\varepsilon^{2}}\,ds.$ (8.22) Remark 8.1 . To describe a rotating dipolar BEC, we only need to include the angular momentum term ( 5.2 ) in the dipolar GPE ( 8.1 ). Therefore, dimensionless rotating dipolar GPEs in 3D and quasi-2D regime are straightforward. 8.3. Theory for ground states In this section, we report results for ground state of dipolar BECs. Denote unit sphere $\displaystyle S=X\cap\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big|\,\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1\right\},$ (8.23) where $X$ is the energy space associated with corresponding potential ( 2.6 ). 8.3.1. 3D case In 3D, the ground state of GPPS ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) is the minimizer of energy $E_{3D}$ ( 8.10 ) over the nonconvex set $S$ [ 23 ] . Theorem 8.1 . Assume $V(\mathbf{x})\geq 0$ for $\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}$ and $\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty}V(\mathbf{x})=\infty$ (i.e., confining
potential) in GPPS ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ), then we have: (i) If $\beta\geq 0$ and $-\frac{1}{2}\beta\leq\lambda\leq\beta$ ,
there exists a ground state $\phi_{g}\in S$ , and the positive ground
state $|\phi_{g}|$ is unique. Moreover, $\phi_{g}=e^{i\theta_{0}}|\phi_{g}|$ for some constant $\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}$ . (ii) If $\beta<0$ , or $\beta\geq 0$ and $\lambda<-\frac{1}{2}\beta$ or $\lambda>\beta$ , there exists no ground state, i.e., $\inf\limits_{\phi\in S}E_{\rm 3D}(\phi)=-\infty$ . By splitting the total energy $E_{\rm 3D}(\cdot)$ in ( 8.10 ) into
kinetic, potential, interaction and dipolar energies, i.e. $\displaystyle E_{3D}(\phi)=E_{\rm kin}(\phi)+E_{\rm pot}(\phi)+E_{\rm int}(\phi)+E_{\rm dip}(\phi),$ (8.24) where $\displaystyle E_{\rm kin}(\phi)=$ $\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|\nabla\phi(\mathbf{x})|^{2}d\mathbf{x},\ E_{\rm pot}(\phi)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}V(\mathbf{x})|\phi(\mathbf{x})|^{2}d\mathbf{x},\ E_{\rm int}(\phi)=\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|\phi(\mathbf{x})|^{4}d\mathbf{x},$ $\displaystyle E_{\rm dip}(\phi)=$ $\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\left(U_{\rm dip}\ast|\phi|^{2}\right)|\phi(\mathbf{x})|^{2}d\mathbf{x}=\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|\phi(\mathbf{x})|^{2}\left[-|\phi(\mathbf{x})|^{2}-3\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}\varphi\right]d\mathbf{x}$ (8.25) $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\left[-|\phi(\mathbf{x})|^{4}+3(\nabla^{2}\varphi)(\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}\varphi)\right]d\mathbf{x}=\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\left[-|\phi(\mathbf{x})|^{4}+3\left|\partial_{\bf n}\nabla\varphi\right|^{2}\right]d\mathbf{x},$ with $\varphi=\frac{1}{4\pi|\mathbf{x}|}*|\phi|^{2}$ , we have the following
Viral identity [ 23 ] : Proposition 8.1 . Suppose $V(\lambda\mathbf{x})=\lambda^{2}V(\mathbf{x})$ for all $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$ and $\phi_{g}$ is the ground state of a dipolar BEC, i.e., the minimizer of energy
( 8.10 )
under the normalization constraint ( 8.9 ), then we have $\displaystyle 2E_{\rm kin}(\phi_{e})-2E_{\rm pot}(\phi_{e})+3E_{\rm int}(\phi_{e})+3E_{\rm dip}(\phi_{e})=0.$ (8.26) 8.3.2. Quasi-2D case I Associated to the quasi-2D equation I ( 8.15 )-( 8.16 ),
the energy is $\displaystyle E_{\rm 2D}(\phi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}+V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})|\phi|^{2}+\beta_{2D}|\phi|^{4}-\frac{3\lambda}{4}|\phi|^{2}\widetilde{\varphi^{2D}}\right]\,d\mathbf{x}_{\perp},\quad\phi\in X,$ (8.27) where $\beta_{2D}=\frac{\beta-\lambda+3\lambda n_{3}^{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\varepsilon}$ and $\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi^{2D}}=\left(\partial_{{\bf n}_{\perp}{\bf n}_{\perp}}-n_{3}^{2}\nabla^{2}\right)\varphi^{2D},\qquad\varphi^{2D}=U_{\varepsilon}^{2D}*|\phi|^{2}.$ (8.28) The ground state $\phi_{g}\in S$ of ( 8.15 ) is the minimizer
of the nonconvex minimization problem [ 18 ] : $\displaystyle\mbox{Find }\phi_{g}\in S,\quad\mbox{such that }E_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{g})=\min\limits_{\phi\in S}E_{\rm 2D}(\phi).$ (8.29) Theorem 8.2 . Assume $0\leq V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})$ and $\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}_{\perp}|\to\infty}V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})=\infty$ , then
we have (i) There exists a ground state $\phi_{g}\in S$ of the system
( 8.15 )-( 8.16 ) if one of the following conditions holds (A $1$ ) $\lambda\geq 0$ and $\beta-\lambda>-\sqrt{2\pi}C_{b}\,\varepsilon$ ; (A $2$ ) $\lambda<0$ and $\beta+\frac{1}{2}(1+3|2n_{3}^{2}-1|)\lambda>-\sqrt{2\pi}C_{b}\,\varepsilon$ , where $C_{b}$ is given in ( 2.12 ). (ii) The positive ground state $|\phi_{g}|$ is unique under one of the
following conditions: (A1 ′ ) $\lambda\geq 0$ and $\beta-\lambda\geq 0$ ; (A2 ′ ) $\lambda<0$ and $\beta+\frac{1}{2}(1+3|2n_{3}^{2}-1|)\lambda\geq 0$ . Moreover, any ground state is of the form $\phi_{g}=e^{i\theta_{0}}|\phi_{g}|$ for some constant $\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}$ . (iii) If $\beta+\frac{1}{2}\lambda(1-3n_{3}^{2})<-\sqrt{2\pi}C_{b}\,\varepsilon$ ,
there exists no ground state of Eq. ( 8.15 ). 8.3.3. Quasi-2D case II Associated to the quasi-2D equation II ( 8.19 ), the energy is $\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}(\phi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}+V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})|\phi|^{2}+\beta_{2D}|\phi|^{4}-\frac{3\lambda}{4}|\phi|^{2}\varphi\right]\,d\mathbf{x},\;\phi\in X,$ (8.30) where $\displaystyle\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})=\left(\partial_{{\bf n}_{\perp}{\bf n}_{\perp}}-n_{3}^{2}\nabla^{2}\right)((-\nabla^{2})^{-1/2}|\phi|^{2}).$ (8.31) The ground state $\phi_{g}\in S$ of the equation ( 8.19 )
is defined as the minimizer of the nonconvex minimization problem: $\displaystyle\mbox{Find }\phi_{g}\in S,\quad\mbox{such that
}\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{g})=\min\limits_{\phi\in S}\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}(\phi).$ (8.32) For the above ground state, we have the following results [ 18 ] . Theorem 8.3 . Assume $0\leq V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})$ and $\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}_{\perp}|\to\infty}V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})=\infty$ , then we have (i) There exists a ground state $\phi_{g}\in S$ of the equation
( 8.19 ) if one of the following conditions holds (B1) $\lambda=0$ and $\beta>-\sqrt{2\pi}C_{b}\,\varepsilon$ ; (B2) $\lambda>0$ , $n_{3}=0$ and $\beta-\lambda>-\sqrt{2\pi}C_{b}\,\varepsilon$ ; (B3) $\lambda<0$ , $n_{3}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}$ and $\beta-(1-3n_{3}^{2})\lambda>-\sqrt{2\pi}C_{b}\,\varepsilon$ . (ii) The positive ground state $|\phi_{g}|$ is unique under one of
the following conditions (B1 ′ ) $\lambda=0$ and $\beta\geq 0$ ; (B2 ′ ) $\lambda>0$ , $n_{3}=0$ and $\beta\geq\lambda$ ; (B3 ′ ) $\lambda<0$ , $n_{3}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}$ and $\beta-(1-3n_{3}^{2})\lambda\geq 0$ . Moreover, any ground state $\phi_{g}=e^{i\theta_{0}}|\phi_{g}|$ for some
constant $\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}$ . (iii) There exists no ground state of the equation ( 8.19 ) if
one of the following conditions holds (B1 ′′ ) $\lambda>0$ and $n_{3}\neq 0$ ; (B2 ′′ ) $\lambda<0$ and $n_{3}^{2}<\frac{1}{2}$ ; (B3 ′′ ) $\lambda=0$ and $\beta<-\sqrt{2\pi}C_{b}\,\varepsilon$ . 8.3.4. Quasi-1D case Associated to the quasi-1D equation ( 8.21 ), the energy is $\displaystyle E_{\rm 1D}(\phi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{z}\phi|^{2}+V_{1}(z)|\phi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{1D}|\phi|^{4}+\frac{3\lambda(1-3n_{3}^{2})}{16\sqrt{2\pi}\,\varepsilon}|\phi|^{2}\varphi\right]\,dz,$ (8.33) where $\beta_{1D}=\frac{\beta+\frac{1}{2}\lambda(1-3n_{3}^{2})}{2\pi\varepsilon^{2}}$ and $\displaystyle\varphi(z)=\partial_{zz}(U_{\varepsilon}^{1D}*|\phi|^{2}),\quad U_{\varepsilon}^{1D}(z)=\frac{2e^{\frac{z^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{|z|}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}}\,ds.$ (8.34) Again, the ground state $\phi_{g}\in S$ of the equation ( 8.21 ) is defined as the minimizer of
the nonconvex minimization problem: $\displaystyle\mbox{Find }\phi_{g}\in S,\quad\mbox{such that }E_{\rm 1D}(\phi_{g})=\min\limits_{\phi\in S}\;E_{\rm 1D}(\phi).$ (8.35) For the above ground state, we have the following results [ 18 ] . Theorem 8.4 . (Existence and uniqueness of ground state) Assume $0\leq V_{1}(z)$ and $\lim_{|z|\to\infty}V_{1}(z)=\infty$ , for any parameter $\beta$ , $\lambda$ and $\varepsilon$ , there exists a ground state $\phi_{g}\in S$ of the quasi-1D equation
( 8.21 )-( 8.22 ), and the positive ground state $|\phi_{g}|$ is unique under one of the following conditions: (C1) $\lambda(1-3n_{3}^{2})\geq 0$ and $\beta-(1-3n_{3}^{2})\lambda\geq 0$ ; (C2) $\lambda(1-3n_{3}^{2})<0$ and $\beta+\frac{\lambda}{2}(1-3n_{3}^{2})\geq 0$ . Moreover, $\phi_{g}=e^{i\theta_{0}}|\phi_{g}|$ for some constant $\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}$ . 8.4. Well-posedness for dynamics In this section, we study the well-posedness for dynamics of dipolar BECs. 8.4.1. 3D case In 3D, we have the following results for GPPS ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) [ 23 ] . Theorem 8.5 . (Well-posedness) Suppose the real-valued trap
potential $V(\mathbf{x})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$ such that $V(\mathbf{x})\geq 0$ for $\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}$ and $D^{\alpha}V(\mathbf{x})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$ for
all $\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{3}$ with $|\alpha|\geq 2$ . For any initial
data $\psi(\mathbf{x},t=0)=\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})\in X$ ,
there exists $T_{\mbox{\rm max}}\in(0,+\infty]$ such that the problem
( 8.7 )-( 8.8 )
has a unique maximal solution $\psi\in C\left([0,T_{\rm max}),X\right)$ . It is maximal in the
sense that if $T_{\rm max}<\infty$ , then $\|\psi(\cdot,t)\|_{X}\to\infty$ when $t\to T^{-}_{\rm max}$ .
Moreover, the mass $N(\psi(\cdot,t))$ and energy $E_{\rm 3D}(\psi(\cdot,t))$ defined in ( 8.9 ) and ( 8.10 ),
respectively, are conserved for $t\in[0,T_{\rm max})$ . Specifically,
if $\beta\geq 0$ and $-\frac{1}{2}\beta\leq\lambda\leq\beta$ , the solution
to ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) is global in time, i.e., $T_{\rm max}=\infty$ . Theorem 8.6 . (Finite time blow-up) If $\beta<0$ , or $\beta\geq 0$ and $\lambda<-\frac{1}{2}\beta$ or $\lambda>\beta$ ,
and assume $V(\mathbf{x})$ satisfies $3V(\mathbf{x})+\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla V(\mathbf{x})\geq 0$ for $\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}$ . For any initial data $\psi(\mathbf{x},t=0)=\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})\in X$ to the problem
( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ), there exists finite time blow-up, i.e., $T_{\rm max}<\infty$ , if one of the following holds: (i) $E_{\rm 3D}(\psi_{0})<0$ ; (ii) $E_{\rm 3D}(\psi_{0})=0$ and ${\rm Im}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{\overline{\psi}}_{0}(\mathbf{x})\ (\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x}))\,d\mathbf{x}\right)<0$ ; (iii) $E_{\rm 3D}(\psi_{0})>0$ and ${\rm Im}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{\overline{\psi}}_{0}(\mathbf{x})\ (\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x}))\,d\mathbf{x}\right)<-\sqrt{3E_{\rm 3D}(\psi_{0})}\|\mathbf{x}\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}}$ . 8.4.2. Quasi-2D case I For quasi-2D equation I ( 8.15 )-( 8.16 ), we have the following results [ 18 ] . Theorem 8.7 . (Well-posedness of Cauchy problem) Suppose the real-valued trap
potential satisfies $V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\geq 0$ for $\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}$ and $\displaystyle V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hbox{ and
}D^{{\bf k}}V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad\hbox{for all }{\bf k}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{2}\ \hbox{with}\ |{\bf k}|\geq 2,$ (8.36) then we have (i) For any initial data $\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t=0)=\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\in X$ ,
there exists a $T_{\rm max}\in(0,+\infty]$ such that the problem
( 8.15 )-( 8.16 )
has a unique maximal solution $\phi\in C\left([0,T_{\rm max}),X\right)$ . It is maximal in
the sense that if $T_{\rm max}<\infty$ , then $\|\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{X}\to\infty$ when $t\to T^{-}_{\rm max}$ . (ii) As long as the solution $\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t)$ remains in the energy
space $X$ , the $L^{2}$ -norm $\|\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{2}$ and energy $E_{\rm 2D}(\phi(\cdot,t))$ in ( 8.27 ) are conserved for $t\in[0,T_{\rm max})$ . (iii) Under either condition (A1) or (A2) in Theorem 8.2 with constant $C_{b}$ being replaced by $C_{b}/\|\phi_{0}\|_{2}^{2}$ ,
the solution of ( 8.15 )-( 8.16 ) is global in time, i.e., $T_{\rm max}=\infty$ . Theorem 8.8 . (Finite time blow-up) For any initial data $\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t=0)=\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\in X$ with $\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathbf{x}_{\perp}|^{2}|\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}_{\perp}<\infty$ , if conditions (A1) and (A2)
with constant $C_{b}$ being replaced by $C_{b}/\|\phi_{0}\|_{2}^{2}$ are not satisfied and assume $V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})$ satisfies $2V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})+\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\geq 0$ , and let $\phi:=\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t)$ be
the solution of the problem ( 8.15 ), there exists finite time
blow-up, i.e., $T_{\rm{max}}<\infty$ , if $\lambda=0$ , or $\lambda>0$ and $n_{3}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}$ , and one of the following holds: (i) $E_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{0})<0$ ; (ii) $E_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{0})=0$ and ${\rm Im}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\overline{\phi}}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\ (\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp}))\,d\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\right)<0$ ; (iii) $E_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{0})>0$ and ${\rm Im}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\overline{\phi}}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\ (\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp}))\,d\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\right)<-\sqrt{2E_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{0})}\|\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\phi_{0}\|_{2}$ . 8.4.3. Quasi-2D case II For quasi-2D equation II ( 8.19 ),
noticing the nonlinearity $\phi(\partial_{{\bf n}_{\perp}{\bf n}_{\perp}}-n_{3}^{2}\nabla^{2})((-\nabla^{2})^{-1/2}|\phi|^{2})$ is actually a derivative nonlinearity, it would bring
significant difficulty in analyzing the dynamic behavior. The Cauchy problem of the Schrödinger equation with derivative
nonlinearity has been investigated extensively in the literatures [ 115 , 125 ] . We are able to prove the existence results in the energy
space with the special structure of our nonlinearity [ 18 ] . Theorem 8.9 . (Existence for Cauchy problem) Suppose the real potential $V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})$ satisfies ( 8.36 ) and $\lim_{|\mathbf{x}_{\perp}|\to\infty}V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})=\infty$ ,
and initial value $\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\in X$ ,
either condition (B2) or (B3) in Theorem 8.3 holds with
constant $C_{b}$ being replaced by $C_{b}/\|\phi_{0}\|_{2}^{2}$ , then
there exists a solution $\phi\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);X)\cap W^{1,\infty}([0,\infty);X^{\ast})$ for the Cauchy problem of
( 8.19 ). Here $X^{\ast}$ denotes the dual space of $X$ .
Moreover, there holds for $L^{2}$ norm and energy $\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}$ ( 8.30 ) conservation, i.e. $\displaystyle\|\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\quad\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}(\phi(\cdot,t))\leq\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{0}),\quad\forall t\geq 0.$ (8.37) Next, we discuss possible finite time blow-up for the continuous
solutions of the quasi-2D equation II ( 8.19 ). To this
purpose, the following assumptions are introduced: (A) Assumption on the trap and coefficient of the cubic term, i.e. $V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})$ satisfies $3V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})+\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\geq 0$ , $\frac{\beta-\lambda+3\lambda n_{3}^{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\varepsilon}\geq-\frac{C_{b}}{\|\phi_{0}\|_{2}^{2}}$ , with $\phi_{0}$ being the initial data of equation ( 8.19 ); (B) Assumption on the trap and coefficient of the nonlocal term, i.e. $V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})$ satisfies $2V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})+\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla V_{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\geq 0$ , $\lambda=0$ or $\lambda>0$ and $n_{3}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}$ . Theorem 8.10 . (Finite time blow-up)
For any initial data $\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t=0)=\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\in X$ with finite variance $\delta_{V}^{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathbf{x}_{\perp}|^{2}|\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}_{\perp}<\infty$ ,
if conditions (B1), (B2) and (B3) with constant $C_{b}$ being replaced by $C_{b}/\|\phi_{0}\|_{2}^{2}$ are not satisfied,
let $\phi:=\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t)\in C([0,T_{\rm max}),X)$ solution of the problem ( 8.19 ) with $L^{2}$ norm and energy conservation, then there exists finite time
blow-up, i.e., $T_{\rm max}<\infty$ , if one of the following
condition holds: (i) $\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{0})<0$ , and either Assumption (A) or (B) holds; (ii) $\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{0})=0$ and ${\rm Im}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\overline{\phi}}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\ (\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp}))\,d\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\right)<0$ ,
and either Assumption (A) or (B) holds; (iii) $\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{0})>0$ , and ${\rm Im}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\overline{\phi}}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\ (\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp}))\,d\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\right)<-(3\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}^{0})^{1/2}\delta_{V}^{0}$ if Assumption (A)
holds, or ${\rm Im}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\overline{\phi}}_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\ (\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\cdot\nabla\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp}))\,d\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\right)<-(2\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}^{0})^{1/2}\delta_{V}^{0}$ if Assumption (B)
holds. Here $\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}^{0}=\tilde{E}_{\rm 2D}(\phi_{0})$ . 8.4.4. Quasi-1D case Concerning the Cauchy problem, similar
results as Theorem 8.7 can be obtained for the equation
( 8.21 ) [ 18 ] . Theorem 8.11 . (Well-posedness for Cauchy problem) Suppose the real-valued trap
potential satisfies $V_{1}(z)\geq 0$ for $z\in{\mathbb{R}}$ and $V_{1}(z)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})$ , $D^{k}V_{1}(z)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})$ for all
integers $k\geq 2$ , for any initial data $\phi(z,t=0)=\phi_{0}(z)\in X$ ,
there exists
a unique solution $\phi\in C\left([0,\infty),X\right)\cap C^{1}\left([0,\infty),X^{\ast}\right)$ to the Cauchy problem of equation ( 8.21 ). 8.5. Convergence rate of dimension reduction In this section, we discuss the dimension reduction of 3D GPPS to
lower dimensions. In lower dimensions,
we require that in the quasi-2D case, $\beta=O(\varepsilon)$ , $\lambda=O(\varepsilon)$ , and in
the quasi-1D case, $\beta=O(\varepsilon^{2})$ , $\lambda=O(\varepsilon^{2})$ , i.e. we are considering
the weak interaction regime, then we would get an $\varepsilon$ -independent limiting equation.
In this regime, we will see that GPPS will reduce to a regular GPE in lower dimensions [ 18 ] . 8.5.1. Reduction to 2D We consider the weak interaction regime, i.e., $\beta\to\varepsilon\beta$ , $\lambda\to\varepsilon\lambda$ .
In Case I ( 8.12 ), for full 3D GPPS
( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ), introduce the re-scaling $z\to\varepsilon z$ , $\psi\to\varepsilon^{-1/2}\psi^{\varepsilon}$ which preserves the normalization,
then $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\psi^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z,t)=\left[\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{x}_{\perp}}^{V}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbf{H}_{z}+(\beta-\lambda)|\psi^{\varepsilon}|^{2}-3\varepsilon\lambda\partial_{{\bf n}_{\varepsilon}{\bf n}_{\varepsilon}}\varphi^{\varepsilon}\right]\psi^{\varepsilon},$ (8.38) where $\mathbf{x}_{\perp}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ and $\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{x}_{\perp}}^{V}=-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy})+V_{2}(x,y),\qquad\mathbf{H}_{z}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{zz}+\frac{z^{2}}{2},$ (8.39) $\displaystyle{\bf n}_{\varepsilon}=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}/\varepsilon),\qquad\partial_{{\bf n}_{\varepsilon}}={\bf n}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla,\qquad\partial_{{\bf n}_{\varepsilon}{\bf n}_{\varepsilon}}=\partial_{{\bf n}_{\varepsilon}}(\partial_{{\bf n}_{\varepsilon}}),$ (8.40) $\displaystyle(-\partial_{xx}-\partial_{yy}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\partial_{zz})\varphi^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}|\psi^{\varepsilon}|^{2},\qquad\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty}\varphi^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z,t)=0.$ (8.41) It is well-known that $\mathbf{H}_{z}$ has eigenvalues $\mu_{k}=k+1/2$ with corresponding
eigenfunction $w_{k}(z)$ ( $k=0,1,\ldots$ ), where $\{w_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}$ forms an
orthornormal basis of $L^{2}(\mathbb{R})$ [ 174 ] , specifically, $w_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi^{1/4}}e^{-z^{2}/2}$ . It is convenient to
consider the initial data concentrated on the ground mode of $\mathbf{H}_{z}$ , i.e., $\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z,0)=\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})w_{0}(z),\quad\phi_{0}\in X(\mathbb{R}^{2})\mbox{ and }\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=1.$ (8.42) In Case I ( 8.12 ),
when $\varepsilon\to 0^{+}$ , quasi-2D equation I ( 8.15 ), II ( 8.19 )
will yield an $\varepsilon$ -independent equation in the weak interaction regime, $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},,t)=\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{x}_{\perp}}^{V}\phi+\frac{\beta-(1-3n_{3}^{2})\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi}}|\phi|^{2}\phi,\quad\mathbf{x}_{\perp}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},$ (8.43) with initial condition $\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},0)=\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})$ . Theorem 8.12 . (Dimension reduction to 2D) Suppose $V_{2}$ satisfies
condition ( 8.36 ), $-\frac{\beta}{2}\leq\lambda\leq\beta$ and $\beta\geq 0$ , let $\psi^{\varepsilon}\in C([0,\infty);X(\mathbb{R}^{3}))$ and $\phi\in C([0,\infty);X(\mathbb{R}^{2}))$ be the unique solutions of equations ( 8.38 )-( 8.42 )
and ( 8.43 ), respectively, then for any $T>0$ , there exists $C_{T}>0$ such that $\displaystyle\left\|\psi^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z,t)-e^{-i\frac{\mu_{0}t}{\varepsilon^{2}}}\phi(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},t)w_{0}(z)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C_{T}\,\varepsilon,\qquad\forall t\in[0,T].$ (8.44) 8.5.2. Reduction to 1D In this case, we again consider the weak interaction regime $\beta\to\varepsilon^{2}\beta$ , $\lambda\to\varepsilon^{2}\lambda$ .
In Case II ( 8.13 ), for the full 3D GPPS
( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ), introducing the re-scaling $x\to\varepsilon x$ , $y\to\varepsilon y$ , $\psi\to\varepsilon^{-1}\psi^{\varepsilon}$ which preserves the
normalization, then $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\psi^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z,t)=\left[\mathbf{H}_{z}^{V}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{x}_{\perp}}+(\beta-\lambda)|\psi^{\varepsilon}|^{2}-3\varepsilon\lambda\partial_{\tilde{{\bf n}}_{\varepsilon}\tilde{{\bf n}}_{\varepsilon}}\varphi^{\varepsilon}\right]\psi^{\varepsilon},$ (8.45) where $\mathbf{x}_{\perp}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ , $z\in\mathbb{R}$ and $\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{z}^{V}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{zz}+V_{1}(z),\quad\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{x}_{\perp}}=-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy}+x^{2}+y^{2}),$ (8.46) $\displaystyle\tilde{{\bf n}}_{\varepsilon}=(n_{1}/\varepsilon,n_{2}/\varepsilon,n_{3}),\quad\partial_{\tilde{{\bf n}}_{\varepsilon}}=\tilde{{\bf n}}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla,\quad\partial_{\tilde{{\bf n}}_{\varepsilon}\tilde{{\bf n}}_{\varepsilon}}=\partial_{\tilde{{\bf n}}_{\varepsilon}}(\partial_{\tilde{{\bf n}}_{\varepsilon}}),$ (8.47) $\displaystyle(-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\partial_{xx}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\partial_{yy}-\partial_{zz})\varphi^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}|\psi^{\varepsilon}|^{2},\qquad\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty}\varphi^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z,t)=0.$ (8.48) Note that the ground state mode of $\mathbf{H}_{\mathbf{x}_{\perp}}$ is given by $w_{0}(x)w_{0}(y)$ with eigenvalue 1,
and the initial data is then assumed to be $\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z,0)=\phi_{0}(z)w_{0}(x)w_{0}(y),\quad\phi_{0}\in X(\mathbb{R})\mbox{ and }\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1.$ (8.49) In Case II ( 8.13 ),
when $\varepsilon\to 0^{+}$ , the quasi-1D equation ( 8.21 ) will lead to an $\varepsilon$ -independent equation in the weak interaction regime, $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\phi(z,t)=\mathbf{H}_{z}^{V}\phi+\frac{\beta+\frac{1}{2}\lambda(1-3n_{3}^{2})}{2\pi}|\phi|^{2}\phi,\qquad z\in\mathbb{R},\quad t>0,$ (8.50) with the initial condition $\phi(z,0)=\phi_{0}(z)$ . We can prove the following results [ 18 ] . Theorem 8.13 . (Dimension reduction to 1D)
Suppose the real-valued trap
potential satisfies $V_{1}(z)\geq 0$ for $z\in{\mathbb{R}}$ and $V_{1}(z)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})$ , $D^{k}V_{1}(z)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})$ for all $k\geq 2$ . Assume $-\frac{\beta}{2}\leq\lambda\leq\beta$ and $\beta\geq 0$ , and let $\psi^{\varepsilon}\in C([0,\infty);X(\mathbb{R}^{3}))$ and $\phi\in C([0,\infty);X(\mathbb{R}))$ be the unique solutions of the equations
( 8.45 )-( 8.49 ) and ( 8.50 ),
respectively, then for any $T>0$ , there exists $C_{T}>0$ such that $\displaystyle\left\|\psi^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z,t)-e^{-it/\varepsilon^{2}}\phi(z,t)w_{0}(x)w_{0}(y)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C_{T}\,\varepsilon,\qquad\forall t\in[0,T].$ (8.51) 8.6. Numerical methods for computing ground states In this section, we present efficient and accurate numerical methods for computing ground states of dipolar BECs, based on the new formulation GPPS ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) of dipolar GPE ( 8.4 ) in 3D. The difficulty of computing dipolar GPE mainly comes from the dipolar term.
In most of the numerical
methods used in the literatures for theoretically and/or numerically
studying the ground states of dipolar BECs, the way to
deal with the convolution in ( 8.4 ) is usually to use the
Fourier transform [ 154 , 186 ] .
However, due to the high singularity in the dipolar interaction
potential ( 8.5 ), there are two drawbacks in these numerical
methods: (i) the Fourier transforms of the dipolar interaction
potential ( 8.5 ) and the density function $|\psi|^{2}$ are
usually carried out in the continuous level on the whole space ${\mathbb{R}}^{3}$ and in the discrete
level on a bounded computational domain $U$ , respectively, and due
to this mismatch, there is a locking phenomena in practical
computation as observed in [ 154 ] ; (ii) the second term in the
Fourier transform of the dipolar interaction potential is $\frac{0}{0}$ -type for $0$ -mode, i.e when $\xi=0$ (see
( 8.11 ) for details), and it is artificially omitted when $\xi=0$ in practical computation [ 154 , 188 , 186 ] thus this may cause
some numerical problems too. With formulation ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ),
new numerical methods for computing ground states and
dynamics of dipolar BECs can be constructed, which can avoid the above two drawbacks and
thus they are more accurate than those currently used in the
literatures. Based on the new mathematical formulation for the energy associated with GPPS ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) in
( 8.10 ), we will present an efficient and accurate backward
Euler sine pseudospectral (BESP) method for computing the ground states of
a dipolar BEC. In practice, the whole space problem is usually truncated into a
bounded computational domain $U=[a,b]\times[c,d]\times[e,f]$ with
homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition. We adopt the method
of gradient flow with discrete normalization (GFDN) as in section 3 : choose a time step $\tau>0$ and set $t_{n}=n\;\tau$ for $n=0,1,\ldots$ Applying
the steepest decent method to the energy functional $E_{\rm 3D}(\phi)$ in
( 8.10 ) without the normalization constraint ( 8.9 ), and then
projecting the solution back to the unit sphere $S$ at the end of
each time interval $[t_{n},t_{n+1}]$ in order to satisfy the
constraint ( 8.9 ). Then GFDN for computing ground state of the GPPS ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) is [ 23 ] : $\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi(\mathbf{x},t)=\left[\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}-V(\mathbf{x})-(\beta-\lambda)|\phi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}+3\lambda\partial_{\bf{nn}}\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)\right]\phi(\mathbf{x},t),\qquad$ (8.52) $\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)=-|\phi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2},\qquad\qquad\mathbf{x}\in U,\quad t_{n}\leq t<t_{n+1},$ (8.53) $\displaystyle\phi(\mathbf{x},t_{n+1}):=\phi(\mathbf{x},t_{n+1}^{+})=\frac{\phi(\mathbf{x},t_{n+1}^{-})}{\|\phi(\cdot,t_{n+1}^{-})\|_{2}},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in U,\quad n\geq 0,$ (8.54) $\displaystyle\left.\phi(\mathbf{x},t)\right|_{\mathbf{x}\in\partial U}=\left.\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)\right|_{\mathbf{x}\in\partial U}=0,\qquad t\geq 0,$ (8.55) $\displaystyle\phi(\mathbf{x},0)=\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x}),\qquad\qquad\hbox{with}\quad\|\phi_{0}\|_{2}=1;$ (8.56) where $\phi(\mathbf{x},t_{n}^{\pm})=\lim\limits_{t\to t_{n}^{\pm}}\phi(\mathbf{x},t)$ . Let $M$ , $K$ and $L$ be even positive integers and define the index
sets $\displaystyle{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}=\{(j,k,l)\ |\ j=1,2,\ldots,M-1,\ k=1,2,\ldots,K-1,\ l=1,2,\ldots,L-1\},$ $\displaystyle{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0}=\{(j,k,l)\ |\ j=0,1,\ldots,M,\ k=0,1,\ldots,K,\ l=0,1,\ldots,L\}.$ Choose the spatial mesh sizes as $h_{x}=\frac{b-a}{M}$ , $h_{y}=\frac{d-c}{K}$ and $h_{z}=\frac{f-e}{L}$ and
define $x_{j}:=a+j\;h_{x},\qquad y_{k}=c+k\;h_{y},\qquad z_{l}=e+l\;h_{z},\qquad(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}^{0}_{MKL}.$ Denote the
space $Y_{MKL}={\rm span}\{\Phi_{jkl}(\mathbf{x}),\quad(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}\},$ with $\displaystyle\Phi_{jkl}(\mathbf{x})=\sin\left(\mu_{j}^{x}(x-a)\right)\sin\left(\mu_{k}^{y}(y-c)\right)\sin\left(\mu_{l}^{z}(z-e)\right),\quad\mathbf{x}\in U,$ (8.57) $\mu_{j}^{x}=\frac{\pi j}{b-a},\qquad\mu_{k}^{y}=\frac{\pi k}{d-c},\qquad\mu_{l}^{z}=\frac{\pi l}{f-e},\qquad(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL};$ and $P_{MKL}:Y=\{\varphi\in C(U)\ |\ \varphi(\mathbf{x})|_{\mathbf{x}\in\partial U}=0\}\to Y_{MKL}$ be the standard project operator, i.e. $(P_{MKL}v)(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{p=1}^{M-1}\sum_{q=1}^{K-1}\sum_{s=1}^{L-1}\widehat{v}_{pqs}\;\Phi_{pqs}(\mathbf{x}),\quad\mathbf{x}\in U,\qquad\forall v\in Y,$ with $\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{pqs}=\int_{U}v(\mathbf{x})\;\Phi_{pqs}(\mathbf{x})\;d\mathbf{x},\qquad(p,q,s)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}.$ (8.58) Then a
backward Euler sine spectral discretization
for ( 8.52 )-( 8.56 ) reads: Find $\phi^{n+1}(\mathbf{x})\in Y_{MKL}$ (i.e. $\phi^{+}(\mathbf{x})\in Y_{MKL}$ ) and $\varphi^{n}(\mathbf{x})\in Y_{MKL}$ such that $\displaystyle\frac{\phi^{+}(\mathbf{x})-\phi^{n}(\mathbf{x})}{\tau}=$ $\displaystyle-P_{MKL}\left\{\left[V(\mathbf{x})+(\beta-\lambda)|\phi^{n}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}-3\lambda\partial_{{\bf{nn}}}\varphi^{n}(\mathbf{x})\right]\phi^{+}(\mathbf{x})\right\}$ $\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\phi^{+}(\mathbf{x}),$ (8.59) $\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\varphi^{n}(\mathbf{x})=$ $\displaystyle-P_{MKL}\left(|\phi^{n}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}\right),\quad\phi^{n+1}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\phi^{+}(\mathbf{x})}{\|\phi^{+}(\mathbf{x})\|_{2}},\quad\mathbf{x}\in U,$ (8.60) where $n\geq 0$ and $\phi^{0}(\mathbf{x})=P_{MKL}\left(\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x})\right)$ is given. The above discretization can be solved in phase space and it is not
suitable in practice due to the difficulty of computing the
integrals in ( 8.58 ). We now present an efficient implementation
by choosing $\phi^{0}(\mathbf{x})$ as the interpolation of $\phi_{0}(\mathbf{x})$ on
the grid points $\{(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l}),\ (j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0}\}$ ,
i.e. $\phi^{0}(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})=\phi_{0}(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})$ for $(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0}$ , and approximating the integrals in ( 8.58 ) by a
quadrature rule on the grid points. Let $\phi_{jkl}^{n}$ and $\varphi_{jkl}^{n}$ be the approximations of $\phi(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l},t_{n})$ and $\varphi(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l},t_{n})$ , respectively, which are the solutions
of ( 8.52 )-( 8.56 ); denote $V_{jkl}=V(x_{j},y_{k}.z_{l})$ , $\rho_{jkl}^{n}=|\phi^{n}_{jkl}|^{2}$ and choose $\phi_{jkl}^{0}=\phi_{0}(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})$ for $(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0}$ . For $n=0,1,\ldots$ , a backward Euler sine
pseudospectral (BESP) discretization for ( 8.52 )-( 8.56 ) reads [ 23 ] : $\displaystyle\frac{\phi_{jkl}^{+}-\phi_{jkl}^{n}}{\tau}=-\left[V_{jkl}+(\beta-\lambda)\left|\phi_{jkl}^{n}\right|^{2}-3\lambda\left.\left(\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}^{s}\varphi^{n}\right)\right|_{jkl}\right]\phi^{+}_{jkl}$ $\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\left.\left(\nabla_{s}^{2}\phi^{+}\right)\right|_{jkl},\quad(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL},$ (8.61) $\displaystyle-\left.\left(\nabla_{s}^{2}\varphi^{n}\right)\right|_{jkl}=|\phi_{j,k,l}^{n}|^{2}=\rho_{jkl}^{n},\quad\phi_{jkl}^{n+1}=\frac{\phi_{jkl}^{+}}{\|\phi^{+}\|_{2}},\quad(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL},$ (8.62) $\displaystyle\phi_{0kl}^{n+1}=\phi_{Mkl}^{n+1}=\phi_{j0l}^{n+1}=\phi_{jKl}^{n+1}=\phi_{jk0}^{n+1}=\phi_{jkL}^{n+1}=0,\quad(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0},$ (8.63) $\displaystyle\varphi_{0kl}^{n}=\varphi_{Mkl}^{n}=\varphi_{j0l}^{n}=\varphi_{jKl}^{n}=\varphi_{jk0}^{n}=\varphi_{jkL}^{n}=0,\quad(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0};$ (8.64) where $\nabla_{s}^{2}$ and $\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}^{s}$ are sine pseudospectral
approximations of $\nabla^{2}$ and $\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}$ , respectively,
defined for $(j,k,l)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{MKL}$ as $\displaystyle\left.\left(\nabla_{s}^{2}\phi^{n}\right)\right|_{jkl}=-\sum_{p=1}^{M-1}\sum_{q=1}^{K-1}\sum_{s=1}^{L-1}\lambda_{pqs}\widetilde{(\phi^{n})}_{pqs}\sin\left(\frac{jp\pi}{M}\right)\sin\left(\frac{kq\pi}{K}\right)\sin\left(\frac{ls\pi}{L}\right),$ $\displaystyle\left.\left(\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}^{s}\varphi^{n}\right)\right|_{jkl}=\sum_{p=1}^{M-1}\sum_{q=1}^{K-1}\sum_{s=1}^{L-1}\frac{\widetilde{(\rho^{n})}_{pqs}}{(\mu_{p}^{x})^{2}+(\mu_{q}^{y})^{2}+(\mu_{s}^{z})^{2}}\left.\left(\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}\Phi_{pqs}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right|_{(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})},$ (8.65) with $\lambda_{pqs}=(\mu_{p}^{x})^{2}+(\mu_{q}^{y})^{2}+(\mu_{s}^{z})^{2}$ , $\widetilde{(\phi^{n})}_{pqs}$ ( $(p,q,s)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL})$ the
discrete sine transform coefficients of the vector $\phi^{n}$ for $(p,q,s)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKl}$ as $\displaystyle\widetilde{(\phi^{n})}_{pqs}=\frac{8}{MKL}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\sum_{l=1}^{L-1}\phi^{n}_{jkl}\sin\left(\frac{jp\pi}{M}\right)\sin\left(\frac{kq\pi}{K}\right)\sin\left(\frac{ls\pi}{L}\right),$ (8.66) and the discrete norm is defined as $\|\phi^{+}\|_{2}^{2}=h_{x}h_{y}h_{z}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\sum_{l=1}^{L-1}|\phi_{jkl}^{+}|^{2}.$ Similar as those in section 3.3 (cf. [ 25 ] ), the linear
system ( 8.61 )-( 8.64 ) can be iteratively
solved in phase space very efficiently via discrete sine transform
and we omit the details here for brevity. 8.7. Time splitting scheme for dynamics Similarly, based on the new Gross-Pitaevskii-Poisson type system
( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ), we will present an efficient and
accurate time-splitting sine pseudospectral (TSSP) method for
computing the dynamics of a dipolar BEC. Again, in practice, the whole space problem is truncated into a
bounded computational domain $U=[a,b]\times[c,d]\times[e,f]$ with
homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition. From time $t=t_{n}$ to time $t=t_{n+1}$ , the Gross-Pitaevskii-Poisson type system
( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) is solved in two steps. One solves
first $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\psi(\mathbf{x},t),\quad\mathbf{x}\in U,\quad\left.\psi(\mathbf{x},t)\right|_{\mathbf{x}\in\partial U}=0,\quad t_{n}\leq t\leq t_{n+1},$ (8.67) for the time step of length $\tau$ , followed by
solving $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)=\left[V(\mathbf{x})+(\beta-\lambda)|\psi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}-3\lambda\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)\right]\psi(\mathbf{x},t),$ (8.68) $\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)=-|\psi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in U,\qquad t_{n}\leq t\leq t_{n+1};$ (8.69) $\displaystyle\left.\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)\right|_{\mathbf{x}\in\partial U}=0,\qquad\left.\psi(\mathbf{x},t)\right|_{\mathbf{x}\in\partial U}=0,\qquad t_{n}\leq t\leq t_{n+1};$ (8.70) for the same time step. Eq. ( 8.67 ) will be discretized in
space by sine pseudospectral method and integrated in time exactly . For $t\in[t_{n},t_{n+1}]$ , the equations
( 8.68 )-( 8.70 ) leave $|\psi|$ and $\varphi$ invariant
in $t$ and therefore they collapses
to $\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)=\left[V(\mathbf{x})+(\beta-\lambda)|\psi(\mathbf{x},t_{n})|^{2}-3\lambda\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}\varphi(\mathbf{x},t_{n})\right]\psi(\mathbf{x},t),\ t_{n}\leq t\leq t_{n+1},$ (8.71) $\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\varphi(\mathbf{x},t_{n})=-|\psi(\mathbf{x},t_{n})|^{2},\hskip 18.49988pt\mathbf{x}\in U.$ (8.72) Again, equation ( 8.72 ) will be discretized in
space by sine pseudospectral method [ 47 , 164 , 165 , 23 ] and the linear
ODE ( 8.71 ) can be integrated in time exactly . Let $\psi_{jkl}^{n}$ and $\varphi_{jkl}^{n}$ be the approximations of $\psi(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l},t_{n})$ and $\varphi(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l},t_{n})$ ,
respectively, which are the solutions of ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 );
and choose $\psi^{0}_{jkl}=\psi_{0}(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})$ for $(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0}$ . For $n=0,1,\ldots$ , a second-order TSSP method for
solving ( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) via the standard Strang
splitting is [ 23 ] $\displaystyle\psi^{(1)}_{jkl}=\sum_{p=1}^{M-1}\sum_{q=1}^{K-1}\sum_{s=1}^{L-1}e^{-i\tau\frac{(\mu_{p}^{x})^{2}+(\mu_{q}^{y})^{2}+(\mu_{r}^{z})^{2}}{4}}\;\widetilde{(\psi^{n})}_{pqs}\sin\left(\frac{jp\pi}{M}\right)\sin\left(\frac{kq\pi}{K}\right)\sin\left(\frac{ls\pi}{L}\right),$ $\displaystyle\psi^{(2)}_{jkl}=e^{-i\tau\left[V(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})+(\beta-\lambda)|\psi_{jkl}^{(1)}|^{2}-3\lambda\left.\left(\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}^{s}\varphi^{(1)}\right)\right|_{jkl}\right]}\;\psi_{jkl}^{(1)},\hskip 9.24994pt(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0},$ (8.73) $\displaystyle\psi^{n+1}_{jkl}=\sum_{p=1}^{M-1}\sum_{q=1}^{K-1}\sum_{s=1}^{L-1}e^{-i\tau\frac{(\mu_{p}^{x})^{2}+(\mu_{q}^{y})^{2}+(\mu_{r}^{z})^{2}}{4}}\;\widetilde{(\psi^{(2)})}_{pqs}\sin\left(\frac{jp\pi}{M}\right)\sin\left(\frac{kq\pi}{K}\right)\sin\left(\frac{ls\pi}{L}\right);$ where $\widetilde{(\psi^{n})}_{pqs}$ and $\widetilde{(\psi^{(2)})}_{pqs}$ ( $(p,q,s)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}$ ) are
the discrete sine transform coefficients of the vectors $\psi^{n}$ and $\psi^{(2)}$ , respectively (defined similarly as those in
( 8.66 )); and $\left.\left(\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}^{s}\varphi^{(1)}\right)\right|_{jkl}$ can be computed as in ( 8.65 ) with $\rho^{n}_{jkl}=\rho^{(1)}_{jkl}:=|\psi^{(1)}_{jkl}|^{2}$ for $(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0}$ . The above method is explicit and unconditionally stable. The memory
cost is $O(MKL)$ and the computational cost per time step is $O\left(MKL\ln(MKL)\right)$ . 8.8. Numerical results In this section, we first compare our new
methods and the standard method used in the literatures [ 188 , 186 ] to evaluate numerically the dipolar
energy and then report ground states and dynamics of dipolar BECs by
using our new numerical methods. Let $\displaystyle\phi:=\phi(\mathbf{x})=\pi^{-3/4}\gamma_{x}^{1/2}\gamma_{z}^{1/4}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\gamma_{x}(x^{2}+y^{2})+\gamma_{z}z^{2}\right)},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.$ (8.74) Then the
dipolar energy $E_{\rm dip}(\phi)$ in ( 8.25 )
can be evaluated analytically as [ 178 ] $\displaystyle E_{\rm dip}(\phi)=-\frac{\lambda\gamma_{x}\sqrt{\gamma_{z}}}{4\pi\sqrt{2\pi}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1+2\chi^{2}}{1-\chi^{2}}-\frac{3\chi^{2}\rm{arctan}\left(\sqrt{\chi^{2}-1}\right)}{(1-\chi^{2})\sqrt{\chi^{2}-1}},&\chi>1,\\
0,&\chi=1,\\
\frac{1+2\chi^{2}}{1-\chi^{2}}-\frac{1.5\chi^{2}}{(1-\chi^{2})\sqrt{1-\chi^{2}}}\rm{ln}\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1-\chi^{2}}}{1-\sqrt{1-\chi^{2}}}\right),&\chi<1,\\
\end{array}\right.$ (8.75) with $\chi=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{z}}{\gamma_{x}}}$ . This provides a
perfect example to test the efficiency of different numerical
methods to deal with the dipolar potential. Based on our new
formulation, the dipolar energy can be evaluated via
discrete sine transform (DST) as $\displaystyle E_{\rm dip}(\phi)\approx\frac{\lambda h_{x}h_{y}h_{z}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\sum_{l=1}^{L-1}|\phi(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})|^{2}\left[-|\phi(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})|^{2}-3\left.\left(\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}^{s}\varphi^{n}\right)\right|_{jkl}\right],$ where $\left.\left(\partial_{{\bf n}{\bf n}}^{s}\varphi^{n}\right)\right|_{jkl}$ is
computed as in ( 8.65 ) with $\rho^{n}_{jkl}=|\phi(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})|^{2}$ for $(j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0}$ . In the literatures [ 188 , 186 ] , this
dipolar energy is usually calculated via discrete Fourier transform
(DFT) as $E_{\rm dip}(\phi)\approx\frac{\lambda h_{x}h_{y}h_{z}}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\sum_{l=0}^{L-1}|\phi(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l})|^{2}\left[{{\mathcal{F}}}^{-1}_{jkl}\left(\widehat{(U_{\rm dip})}(2\mu_{p}^{x},2\mu_{q}^{y},2\mu_{s}^{z})\cdot{{\mathcal{F}}}_{pqs}(|\phi|^{2})\right)\right],$ where ${{\mathcal{F}}}$ and ${{\mathcal{F}}}^{-1}$ are the discrete Fourier and inverse Fourier
transforms over the grid points $\{(x_{j},y_{k},z_{l}),\ (j,k,l)\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{MKL}^{0}\}$ , respectively [ 186 ] . We take $\lambda=8\pi/3$ ,
the bounded computational domain $U=[-16,16]^{3}$ , $M=K=L$ and thus $h=h_{x}=h_{y}=h_{z}=\frac{32}{M}$ . Tab. 8.1 lists the errors $e:=\left|E_{\rm dip}(\phi)-E_{\rm dip}^{h}\right|$ with $E_{\rm dip}^{h}$ computed numerically via either DST ( 8.1 ) or
DFT with mesh size $h$ for three cases: • Case I. $\gamma_{x}=0.25$ and $\gamma_{z}=1$ , $\chi=2.0$ and $E_{\rm dip}(\phi)=0.0386708614$ ; • Case II. $\gamma_{x}=\gamma_{z}=1$ , $\chi=1.0$ and $E_{\rm dip}(\phi)=0$ ; • Case III. $\gamma_{x}=2$ and $\gamma_{z}=1$ , $\chi=\sqrt{0.5}$ and $E_{\rm dip}(\phi)=-0.1386449741$ .
