the dynamics of a dipolar BEC (e.g., 52 Cr [ 147 ] ) by using our numerical method ( 8.73 ). Again, in the computation and
results, we always use the dimensionless quantities. We take the
bounded computational domain $U=[-8,8]^{2}\times[-4,4]$ , $M=K=L=128$ , i.e. $h=h_{x}=h_{y}=1/8,h_{z}=1/16$ , time step $\tau=0.001$ . The initial data $\psi(\mathbf{x},0)=\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})$ is chosen as
the ground state of a dipolar BEC computed numerically by our
numerical method with ${\bf n}=(0,0,1)^{T}$ , $V(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{2}(x^{2}+y^{2}+25z^{2})$ , $\beta=103.58$ and $\lambda=0.8\beta=82.864$ . We study the dynamics of suddenly
changing the dipolar direction from ${\bf n}=(0,0,1)^{T}$ to ${\bf n}=(1,0,0)^{T}$ at $t=0$ and keeping all other quantities unchanged.
Fig. 8.2 depicts the time evolution of the energy $E_{\rm 3D}(t):=E_{\rm 3D}(\psi(\cdot,t))$ , chemical potential $\mu(t)=\mu(\psi(\cdot,t))$ , kinetic energy $E_{\rm kin}(t):=E_{\rm kin}(\psi(\cdot,t))$ , potential energy $E_{\rm pot}(t):=E_{\rm pot}(\psi(\cdot,t))$ , interaction energy $E_{\rm int}(t):=E_{\rm int}(\psi(\cdot,t))$ , dipolar energy $E_{\rm dip}(t):=E_{\rm dip}(\psi(\cdot,t))$ , condensate widths $\sigma_{x}(t):=\sigma_{x}(\psi(\cdot,t))$ , $\sigma_{z}(t):=\sigma_{z}(\psi(\cdot,t))$ , and central density $\rho(t):=|\psi({\bf 0},t)|^{2}$ , as well as the isosurface of the
density function $\rho(\mathbf{x},t):=|\psi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}=0.01$ for different
times. From the above numerical results, we can see that the numerical methods based on the GPPS
( 8.7 )-( 8.8 ) are much more efficient and accurate than those used in the literatures based on ( 8.1 ). 8.9. Extensions in lower dimensions Here, we consider the numerical methods for computing ground states and dynamics for dipolar BECs
in 2D and 1D. The difficulties arise from the nonlocal terms, i.e., the dipolar terms in quasi-2D equation I ( 8.15 ), quasi-2D equation II ( 8.19 ) and quasi-1D equation ( 8.21 ). It is obvious that those methods introduced in sections 3 and 4 can be extended here, provided that the nonlocal terms can be computed properly. We propose to compute the convolution terms in ( 8.15 ), ( 8.19 ) and ( 8.21 ) by Fourier transform. Unlike the 3D case, there are no singularities for convolution kernels at origin, thus discrete Fourier transform is accurate in these cases. Lemma 8.1 . (Kernels $U_{\varepsilon}^{2D}$ in ( 8.16 ) and $U_{\varepsilon}^{1D}$ ( 8.22 ) )
For any real function $f(\mathbf{x})$ in the Schwartz
space ${\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$ , we have $\displaystyle\widehat{U_{\varepsilon}^{2D}*f}(\xi)=\hat{f}(\xi)\,\widehat{U_{\varepsilon}^{2D}}(\xi)=\frac{\hat{f}(\xi)}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{-\varepsilon^{2}s^{2}/2}}{|\xi|^{2}+s^{2}}ds,\qquad f\in{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).$ (8.76) For any $g(z)$ in the Schwartz space ${\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{R})$ , we
have $\displaystyle\widehat{U_{\varepsilon}^{1D}*g}(\xi)=\hat{g}(\xi)\widehat{U_{\varepsilon}^{1D}}(\xi)=\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon\hat{g}(\xi)}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\varepsilon^{2}s/2}}{|\xi|^{2}+s}ds,\quad\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}.$ (8.77) Here $\widehat{f}$ and $\widehat{g}$ denote the Fourier transforms of $f$ and $g$ , respectively. The Fourier transforms of $U_{\varepsilon}^{2D}$ and $U_{\varepsilon}^{1D}$ can be written in terms of the second kind Bessel functions [ 65 ] . 9. Mathematical theory and numerical methods for two component BEC In view of potential applications, such
as the generation of bright beams of coherent matter waves (atom laser),
a central goal in the study of BEC has been the formation of condensate with the
number of atoms being as large as possible. It is thus of particular
interest to study a scenario where this goal is achieved by
uniting two (or more) independently grown condensates to form
one large single condensate. The first experiment involving the
uniting of multiple-component BEC was performed with
atoms evaporatively cooled in the $|F=2,m_{f}=2\rangle$ and $|1,-1\rangle$ spin states of 87 Rb [ 142 ] . It demonstrated the possibility of producing
long-lived multiple condensate systems, and that the condensate
wave function is dramatically affected by the presence of
inter-component interactions. 9.1. Coupled Gross-Pitaevskii equations At temperatures $T$ much smaller than the critical temperature $T_{c}$ [ 150 ] , a two-component BEC with
an internal atomic Josephson junction (or an external driving field) can be well described by the coupled Gross-Pitaevskii equations (CGPEs) [ 134 , 131 , 185 , 195 , 19 ] , $\displaystyle\begin{split}&i\hbar\partial_{t}\psi_{1}=\left[-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+\hbar\delta+g_{11}|\psi_{1}|^{2}+g_{12}|\psi_{2}|^{2}\right]\psi_{1}+\lambda\hbar\psi_{2},\\
&i\hbar\partial_{t}\psi_{2}=\left[-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+g_{21}|\psi_{1}|^{2}+g_{22}|\psi_{2}|^{2}\right]\psi_{2}+\lambda\hbar\psi_{1},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.\end{split}$ (9.1) Here $\Psi:=\Psi(\mathbf{x},t)=(\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t),\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t))^{T}$ is the
complex-valued macroscopic wave function, $V(\mathbf{x})$ is the
real-valued external trapping potential, $\lambda$ is the effective
Rabi frequency describing the strength to realize the internal atomic
Josephson junction (JJ) by a Raman transition, $\delta$ is the Raman transition constant.
The interactions of particles are described by $g_{jl}=\frac{4\pi\hbar^{2}a_{jl}}{m}$ with $a_{jl}=a_{lj}$ ( $j,l=1,2$ ) being
the $s$ -wave scattering lengths between the $j$ th and $l$ th component (positive for
repulsive interaction and negative for attractive interaction).
It is necessary to ensure that the wave function is properly
normalized. Especially, we require $\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\left[|\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}+|\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}\right]\,d\mathbf{x}=N=N_{1}^{0}+N_{2}^{0},$ (9.2) where $N_{j}^{0}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|\psi_{j}(\mathbf{x},0)|^{2}d\mathbf{x},$ is the particle number of the $j$ th ( $j=1,2$ ) component at time $t=0$ and $N$ the total number of particle in
the two-component BEC. By properly nondimensionalization and dimension reduction, we can obtain
the following dimensionless CGPEs in $d$ -dimensions ( $d=1,2,3$ ) for a two-component BEC [ 195 , 19 ] $\displaystyle\begin{split}&i\partial_{t}\psi_{1}=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+\delta+(\beta_{11}|\psi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{12}|\psi_{2}|^{2})\right]\psi_{1}+\lambda\psi_{2},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
&i\partial_{t}\psi_{2}=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+(\beta_{12}|\psi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{22}|\psi_{2}|^{2})\right]\psi_{2}+\lambda\psi_{1},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{split}$ (9.3) Here $\Psi:=\Psi(\mathbf{x},t)=(\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t),\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t))^{T}$ is the dimensionless
complex-valued macroscopic wave function, $V(\mathbf{x})$ is the dimensionless
real-valued external trapping potential, $\beta_{11}$ , $\beta_{12}=\beta_{21}$ , $\beta_{22}$ are dimensionless interaction constants, $\delta$ and $\lambda$ are dimensionless constants.
In addition, the wave function is normalized as $\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left[|\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}+|\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}\right]\,d\mathbf{x}=1.$ (9.4) The dimensionless CGPEs ( 9.3 ) conserves the total mass or
normalization, i.e. $\displaystyle N(t):=\|\Psi(\cdot,t)\|^{2}=N_{1}(t)+N_{2}(t)\equiv\|\Psi(\cdot,0)\|^{2}=1,\quad t\geq 0,$ (9.5) with $\displaystyle N_{j}(t)=\|\psi_{j}(\mathbf{x},t)\|_{2}^{2}:=\|\psi_{j}(\mathbf{x},t)\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\psi_{j}(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x},\quad t\geq 0,\quad j=1,2,$ (9.6) and
the energy $\displaystyle E(\Psi)$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\left(|\nabla\psi_{1}|^{2}+|\nabla\psi_{2}|^{2}\right)+V(\mathbf{x})(|\psi_{1}|^{2}+|\psi_{2}|^{2})+\delta|\psi_{1}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{11}|\psi_{1}|^{4}$ (9.7) $\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{22}|\psi_{2}|^{4}+\beta_{12}|\psi_{1}|^{2}|\psi_{2}|^{2}+2\lambda\cdot\text{Re}(\psi_{1}{\overline{\psi}}_{2})\biggl]d\mathbf{x}.$ In addition, if there is no internal
Josephson junction in ( 9.3 ), i.e. $\lambda=0$ , the mass of each
component is also conserved, i.e. $\displaystyle N_{1}(t)\equiv\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},0)|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}:=\alpha,\quad N_{2}(t)\equiv\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x},0)|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}:=1-\alpha,$ (9.8) with $0\leq\alpha\leq 1$ a given constant. 9.2. Ground states for the case without Josephson junction If there is no external driving field in ( 9.3 ), i.e. $\lambda=0$ ,
for any given $\alpha\in[0,1]$ , the ground state $\Phi_{g}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})=(\phi_{1}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{x}),\phi_{2}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{x}))^{T}$ of the
two-component BEC is defined as the minimizer of the following
nonconvex minimization problem: Find $\left(\Phi_{g}^{\alpha}\in S_{\alpha}\right)$ , such that $E_{g}^{\alpha}:=E_{0}\left(\Phi_{g}^{\alpha}\right)=\min_{\Phi\in S_{\alpha}}E_{0}\left(\Phi\right),$ (9.9) where $S_{\alpha}$ is a nonconvex set defined as $\displaystyle S_{\alpha}:=\left\{\Phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})^{T}\ |\ \|\phi_{1}\|_{2}^{2}=\alpha,\ \|\phi_{2}\|_{2}^{2}=1-\alpha,\ E_{0}(\Phi)<\infty\right\},$ (9.10) and the energy
functional $E_{0}(\Phi)$ is defined as $\displaystyle E_{0}(\Phi)$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\left(|\nabla\phi_{1}|^{2}+|\nabla\phi_{2}|^{2}\right)+V(\mathbf{x})(|\phi_{1}|^{2}+|\phi_{2}|^{2})+\delta|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{11}|\phi_{1}|^{4}$ (9.11) $\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{22}|\phi_{2}|^{4}+\beta_{12}|\phi_{1}|^{2}|\phi_{2}|^{2}\biggl]d\mathbf{x}.$ Again, it is
easy to see that the ground state $\Phi_{g}^{\alpha}$ satisfies the
following Euler-Lagrange equations, $\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu_{1}\,\phi_{1}=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbb{x})+\delta+(\beta_{11}|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{12}|\phi_{2}|^{2})\right]\phi_{1},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\\
&\mu_{2}\,\phi_{2}=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbb{x})+(\beta_{12}|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{22}|\phi_{2}|^{2})\right]\phi_{2},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\end{split}$ (9.12) under the two constraints $\displaystyle\|\phi_{1}\|_{2}^{2}:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}=\alpha,\qquad\|\phi_{2}\|_{2}^{2}:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}=1-\alpha,$ (9.13) with $\mu_{1}$ and $\mu_{2}$ being the Lagrange multipliers or chemical
potentials corresponding to the two constraints ( 9.13 ).
Again, the above time-independent CGPEs ( 9.12 ) can also be
obtained from the CGPEs ( 9.3 ) with $\lambda=0$ by substituting
the ansatz $\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)=e^{-i\mu_{1}t}\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\qquad\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)=e^{-i\mu_{2}t}\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x}).$ (9.14) Considering the case $\alpha\in(0,1)$ in minimization problem ( 9.9 ), denote $\beta_{11}^{\prime}:=\alpha\beta_{11},\quad\beta_{22}^{\prime}:=(1-\alpha)\beta_{22},\quad\beta_{12}^{\prime}:=\sqrt{\alpha(1-\alpha)}\beta_{12},\quad\alpha^{\prime}:=\alpha(1-\alpha),$ and $B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\beta_{11}&\beta_{12}\\
\beta_{21}&\beta_{22}\\
\end{array}\right),\qquad B^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\beta_{11}^{\prime}&\beta_{12}^{\prime}\\
\beta_{21}^{\prime}&\beta_{22}^{\prime}\\
\end{array}\right).$ Then the following conclusions can be drawn [ 19 ] . Theorem 9.1 . (Existence and uniqueness of ( 9.9 ))
Suppose $V(\mathbf{x})\geq 0$ satisfying $\lim_{|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty}V(\mathbf{x})=\infty$ and at least one of the following condition holds, (i) $d=1$ ; (ii) $d=2$ and $\beta^{\prime}_{11}\geq-C_{b}$ , $\beta^{\prime}_{22}\geq-C_{b}$ , and $\beta^{\prime}_{12}\geq-\sqrt{(C_{b}+\beta^{\prime}_{11})(C_{b}+\beta_{22}^{\prime})}$ ; (iii) $d=3$ and $B$ is
either positive semi-definite or nonnegative, then there exists a ground state $\Phi_{g}=(\phi_{1}^{g},\phi_{2}^{g})^{T}$ of
( 9.9 ). In addition, $\widetilde{\Phi}_{g}:=(e^{i\theta_{1}}|\phi_{1}^{g}|,e^{i\theta_{2}}|\phi_{2}^{g}|)$ is also a
ground state of ( 9.9 ) with two constants $\theta_{1}$ and $\theta_{2}$ . Furthermore, if the matrix $B^{\prime}$ is positive
semi-definite, the ground state $(|\phi_{1}^{g}|,|\phi_{2}^{g}|)^{T}$ of
( 9.9 ) is unique.
In contrast, if one of the following conditions holds, (i) $d=2$ and $\beta_{11}^{\prime}<-C_{b}$ or $\beta_{22}^{\prime}<-C_{b}$ or $\beta^{\prime}_{12}<-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\left(\alpha\beta^{\prime}_{11}+(1-\alpha)\beta_{22}^{\prime}+C_{b}\right)$ ; (ii) $d=3$ and $\beta_{11}<0$ or $\beta_{22}<0$ or $\beta_{12}<-\frac{1}{2\alpha^{\prime}}(\alpha^{2}\beta_{11}+(1-\alpha)^{2}\beta_{22})$ . there exists no ground states of ( 9.9 ). 9.3. Ground states for the case with Josephson junction The ground state $\Phi_{g}(\mathbf{x})=(\phi_{1}^{g}(\mathbf{x}),\phi_{2}^{g}(\mathbf{x}))^{T}$ of
the two-component BEC with an internal
Josephson junction ( 9.3 )
is defined as the minimizer of the following nonconvex minimization
problem: Find $\left(\Phi_{g}\in S\right)$ , such that $E_{g}:=E\left(\Phi_{g}\right)=\min_{\Phi\in S}E\left(\Phi\right),$ (9.15) where $S$ is a nonconvex set defined as $\displaystyle S:=\left\{\Phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})^{T}\big|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left(|\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}+|\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}\right)d\mathbf{x}=1,\ E(\Phi)<\infty\right\}.$ (9.16) It is easy to see that the ground state $\Phi_{g}$ satisfies the following Euler-Lagrange equations, $\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mu\,\phi_{1}=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbb{x})+\delta+(\beta_{11}|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{12}|\phi_{2}|^{2})\right]\phi_{1}+\lambda\phi_{2},\quad\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
&\mu\,\phi_{2}=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbb{x})+(\beta_{12}|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{22}|\phi_{2}|^{2})\right]\phi_{2}+\lambda\phi_{1},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\end{split}$ (9.17) under the constraint $\displaystyle\|\Phi\|_{2}^{2}:=\|\Phi\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left[|\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}+|\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}\right]\,d\mathbf{x}=1,$ (9.18) with the eigenvalue $\mu$ being the Lagrange multiplier
or chemical potential corresponding to the constraint ( 9.18 ), which can be
computed as $\displaystyle\mu$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle\mu(\Phi)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\left(|\nabla\phi_{1}|^{2}+|\nabla\phi_{2}|^{2}\right)+V(\mathbf{x})(|\phi_{1}|^{2}+|\phi_{2}|^{2})+\delta|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{11}|\phi_{1}|^{4}$ (9.19) $\displaystyle\qquad+\beta_{22}|\phi_{2}|^{4}+2\beta_{12}|\phi_{1}|^{2}|\phi_{2}|^{2}+2\lambda\cdot\text{Re}(\phi_{1}{\overline{\phi}}_{2})\biggl]d\mathbf{x}.$ In
fact, the above time-independent CGPEs ( 9.17 ) can also be
obtained from the CGPEs ( 9.3 ) by substituting the ansatz $\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)=e^{-i\mu t}\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\qquad\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)=e^{-i\mu t}\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x}).$ (9.20) The eigenfunctions of the nonlinear eigenvalue problem ( 9.17 )
under the normalization ( 9.18 ) are usually called as
stationary states of the two-component BEC ( 9.3 ). Among
them, the eigenfunction with minimum energy is the ground state and
those whose energy are larger than that of the ground state are
usually called as excited states. It is easy to see that the ground state $\Phi_{g}$ defined in
( 9.15 ) is equivalent to the following: Find $\left(\Phi_{g}\in S\right)$ , such that $\displaystyle E\left(\Phi_{g}\right)=\min_{\Phi\in S}E\left(\Phi\right)=\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\;E(\alpha),\qquad E(\alpha)=\min_{\Phi\in S_{\alpha}}\;E(\Phi).$ (9.21) Denote $\mathcal{D}=\left\{\Phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})^{T}\ |\,V\,|\phi_{j}|^{2}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \phi_{j}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ j=1,2\right\},$ (9.22) then the ground state $\Phi_{g}$ of ( 9.15 ) is also given by the following: Find $\left(\Phi_{g}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\right)$ , such that $E_{g}:=E\left(\Phi_{g}\right)=\min_{\Phi\in\mathcal{D}_{1}}E\left(\Phi\right),$ (9.23) where $\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathcal{D}\cap\left\{\Phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})^{T}\ |\ \|\Phi\|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(|\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}+|\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x})|^{2})\,d\mathbf{x}=1\right\}.$ (9.24) In addition, we introduce the auxiliary energy functional $\displaystyle\widetilde{E}(\Phi)=$ $\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\left(|\nabla\phi_{1}|^{2}+|\nabla\phi_{2}|^{2}\right)+\left[V(\mathbf{x})\left(|\phi_{1}|^{2}+|\phi_{2}|^{2}\right)+\delta|\phi_{1}|^{2}\right]$ (9.25) $\displaystyle+\left(\frac{1}{2}\beta_{11}|\phi_{1}|^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{22}|\phi_{2}|^{4}+\beta_{12}|\phi_{1}|^{2}|\phi_{2}|^{2}\right)-2|\lambda|\cdot|\phi_{1}|\cdot|\phi_{2}|\,\bigg\}\,d\mathbf{x},$ and the auxiliary nonconvex minimization problem: Find $\left(\Phi_{g}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\right)$ , such that $\widetilde{E}\left(\Phi_{g}\right)=\min_{\Phi\in\mathcal{D}_{1}}\widetilde{E}\left(\Phi\right).$ (9.26) For $\Phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})^{T}$ , we write $E(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=E(\Phi)$ and $\widetilde{E}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=\widetilde{E}(\Phi)$ . Then we have
the following lemmas [ 19 ] : Lemma 9.1 . For the minimizers $\Phi_{g}(\mathbf{x})=(\phi_{1}^{g}(\mathbf{x}),\phi_{2}^{g}(\mathbf{x}))^{T}$ of the
nonconvex minimization problems ( 9.23 ) and ( 9.26 ),
we have (i). If $\Phi_{g}$ is a minimizer of ( 9.23 ), then $\phi_{1}^{g}(\mathbf{x})=e^{i\theta_{1}}|\phi_{1}^{g}(\mathbf{x})|$ and $\phi_{2}^{g}(\mathbf{x})=e^{i\theta_{2}}|\phi_{2}^{g}(\mathbf{x})|$ with $\theta_{1}$ and $\theta_{2}$ two constants satisfying $\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}$ if $\lambda<0$ ; and $\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}\pm\pi$ if $\lambda>0$ . In
addition, $\widetilde{\Phi}_{g}=\left(e^{i\theta_{3}}\phi_{1}^{g},e^{i\theta_{4}}\phi_{2}^{g}\right)^{T}$ with $\theta_{3}$ and $\theta_{4}$ two constants satisfying $\theta_{3}=\theta_{4}$ if $\lambda<0$ ; and $\theta_{3}=\theta_{4}\pm\pi$ if $\lambda>0$ is also a minimizer of ( 9.23 ). (ii). If $\Phi_{g}$ is a minimizer of ( 9.26 ), then $\phi_{1}^{g}(\mathbf{x})=e^{i\theta_{1}}|\phi_{1}^{g}(\mathbf{x})|$ and $\phi_{2}^{g}(\mathbf{x})=e^{i\theta_{2}}|\phi_{2}^{g}(\mathbf{x})|$ with $\theta_{1}$ and $\theta_{2}$ two constants. In addition, $\widetilde{\Phi}_{g}=\left(e^{i\theta_{3}}\phi_{1}^{g},e^{i\theta_{4}}\phi_{2}^{g}\right)^{T}$ with $\theta_{3}$ and $\theta_{4}$ two constants is also a minimizer of
( 9.26 ). (iii). If $\Phi_{g}$ is a minimizer of ( 9.23 ), then $\Phi_{g}$ is also a minimizer of ( 9.26 ). (iv). If $\Phi_{g}$ is a minimizer of ( 9.26 ), then $\widetilde{\Phi}_{g}=\left(|\phi_{1}^{g}|,-\text{sign}(\lambda)|\phi_{2}^{g}|\right)^{T}$ is a minimizer of ( 9.23 ). For the auxiliary minimization problem ( 9.26 ), we have the following results generalizing
the single component BEC case in section 2 . Theorem 9.2 . (Existence and uniqueness of ( 9.26 ) [ 19 ] )
Suppose $V(\mathbf{x})\geq 0$ satisfying $\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty}V(\mathbf{x})=\infty$ , then there exists a
minimizer $\Phi^{\infty}=(\phi_{1}^{\infty},\phi_{2}^{\infty})^{T}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}$ of
( 9.26 ) if one of the following conditions holds, (i) $d=1$ ; (ii) $d=2$ and $\beta_{11}\geq-C_{b},\beta_{22}\geq-C_{b}$ , $\beta_{12}\geq-C_{b}-\sqrt{C_{b}+\beta_{11}}\sqrt{C_{b}+\beta_{22}}$ ; (iii) $d=3$ and $B$ is
either positive semi-definite or nonnegative, where $C_{b}$ is given in ( 2.12 ).
In addition, if the matrix $B$ is positive semi-definite and at least one of the
parameters $\delta$ , $\lambda$ , $\gamma_{1}$ and $\gamma_{2}$ are nonzero,
then the minimizer $(|\phi_{1}^{\infty}|,|\phi_{2}^{\infty}|)^{T}$ is unique. Combining Theorem 9.2 and Lemma 9.1 , we draw the conclusions [ 19 ] : Theorem 9.3 . (Existence and uniqueness of ( 9.15 ))
Suppose $V(\mathbf{x})\geq 0$ satisfying $\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty}V(\mathbf{x})=\infty$ and at least one of
the following condition holds, (i) $d=1$ ; (ii) $d=2$ and $\beta_{11}\geq-C_{b}$ , $\beta_{22}\geq-C_{b}$ , and $\beta_{12}\geq-C_{b}-\sqrt{C_{b}+\beta_{11}}\sqrt{C_{b}+\beta_{22}}$ ; (iii) $d=3$ and $B$ is
either positive semi-definite or nonnegative, there exists a
ground state $\Phi_{g}=(\phi_{1}^{g},\phi_{2}^{g})^{T}$ of ( 9.15 ). In
addition, $\widetilde{\Phi}_{g}:=(e^{i\theta_{1}}|\phi_{1}^{g}|,e^{i\theta_{2}}|\phi_{2}^{g}|)$ is also a
ground state of ( 9.15 ) with $\theta_{1}$ and $\theta_{2}$ two
constants satisfying $\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}=\pm\pi$ when $\lambda>0$ and $\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}=0$ when $\lambda<0$ , respectively. Furthermore,
if the matrix $B$ is positive semi-definite and at least one of the
parameters $\delta$ , $\lambda$ , $\gamma_{1}$ and $\gamma_{2}$ are nonzero, then the ground state $(|\phi_{1}^{g}|,-{\rm sign}(\lambda)|\phi_{2}^{g}|)^{T}$ is unique.
In contrast, if one of the following conditions holds, (i) $d=2$ and $\beta_{11}<-C_{b}$ or $\beta_{22}<-C_{b}$ or $\beta_{12}<-C_{b}-\sqrt{C_{b}+\beta_{11}}\sqrt{C_{b}+\beta_{22}}$ ; (ii) $d=3$ and $\beta_{11}<0$ or $\beta_{22}<0$ or $\beta_{12}<0$ with $\beta_{12}^{2}>\beta_{11}\beta_{22}$ ; there exists no ground state of ( 9.15 ). When either $|\delta|$ or $|\lambda|$ goes to infinity, the two component ground state problem ( 9.15 ) will collapse to a single component ground state problem in section 2 [ 19 ] . For fixed $\beta_{11}\geq 0$ and $\beta_{22}\geq 0$ , when $\beta_{12}\to\infty$ ,
the phase of two components of the ground state $\Phi_{g}=(\phi_{1}^{g},\phi_{2}^{g})^{T}$ will be segregated [ 63 , 66 , 92 ] , i.e. $\Phi_{g}$ will converge to a state such
that $\phi_{1}^{g}\cdot\phi_{2}^{g}=0$ . Remark 9.1 . If the potential $V(\mathbf{x})$ in the two equations in
( 9.3 ) is chosen to be different in different equations, i.e. $V_{j}(\mathbf{x})$ in the $j$ th ( $j=1,2$ ) equation, and they satisfy $V_{j}(\mathbf{x})\geq 0$ , $\lim\limits_{|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty}V_{j}(\mathbf{x})=\infty$ ( $j=1,2$ ), then the conclusions in the above Lemmas and Theorems 9.2 - 9.3 are still valid under the similar conditions. 9.4. Dynamical properties Well-posedness of Cauchy problem of the CGPEs ( 9.3 ) in energy space is quite similar to that of single GPE (cf. section 2 ), and we omit the results here. If there is no internal Josephson junction, i.e. $\lambda=0$ , the density of each
component is conserved. With an internal Josephson junction, we have
the following lemmas for the dynamics of the
density of each component [ 195 ] : Lemma 9.2 . Suppose $(\psi_{1}({\mathbf{x}},t),\psi_{2}({\mathbf{x}},t))$ is the solution of the
CGPEs ( 9.3 ) with potential $V(\mathbf{x})+\delta$ for the first component $\psi_{1}$ replaced by $V_{1}(\mathbf{x})$ and potential $V(\mathbf{x})$ for the second component $\psi_{2}$ replaced by $V_{2}(\mathbf{x})$ ; then we have, for $j=1,2$ $\displaystyle\ddot{N}_{j}(t)=-2\lambda^{2}\left[2N_{j}(t)-1\right]+F_{j}(t),\qquad t\geq 0,$ (9.27) with initial conditions $\displaystyle N_{j}(0)=N_{j}^{(0)}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\psi_{j}^{0}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}\;d{\mathbf{x}}=\frac{N_{j}^{0}}{N},$ (9.28) $\displaystyle\dot{N}_{j}(0)=N_{j}^{(1)}=2\lambda\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\rm Im}\left[\psi_{j}^{0}(\mathbf{x})\left(\overline{\psi_{k_{j}}^{0}(\mathbf{x})}\right)\right]\;d{\mathbf{x}};$ (9.29) where $k_{1}=2$ , $k_{2}=1$ and for $t\geq 0$ , $\displaystyle F_{j}(t)=\lambda\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left(\overline{\psi_{j}}\psi_{k_{j}}+\psi_{j}\overline{\psi_{k_{j}}}\right)\bigg[V_{k_{j}}({\mathbf{x}})-V_{j}({\mathbf{x}})$ $\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-(\beta_{jj}-\beta_{k_{j}j})|\psi_{j}|^{2}+(\beta_{k_{j}k_{j}}-\beta_{jk_{j}})|\psi_{k_{j}}|^{2}\bigg]d\mathbf{x},\quad t\geq 0.\qquad\qquad$ From this lemma, we have [ 195 ] Lemma 9.3 . If $\delta=0$ and $\beta_{11}=\beta_{12}=\beta_{21}=\beta_{22}$ in ( 9.3 ),
for any initial data $\Psi(\mathbf{x},t=0)=(\psi_{1}^{0}(\mathbf{x}),\psi_{2}^{0}(\mathbf{x}))^{T}$ , we have,
for $t\geq 0$ , $\displaystyle N_{j}(t)=\left\|\psi_{j}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left(N_{j}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\cos(2\lambda t)+\frac{N_{j}^{(1)}}{2\lambda}\sin(2\lambda t)+\frac{1}{2},\quad j=1,2.$ (9.30) Thus in this case, the density of each component is a periodic
function with period $T=\pi/|\lambda|$ depending only on $\lambda$ . 9.5. Numerical methods for computing ground states To find the ground state, we first present a continuous normalized gradient flow (CNGF) method discussed in section 3.1 and then propose a GFDN method based on discretization of CNGF. 9.5.1. Continuous normalized gradient flow and its discretization In order to compute the ground state of two-component BEC with an
internal Josephson junction ( 9.15 ), we construct the following CNGF [ 19 ] : $\displaystyle\begin{split}&\qquad\frac{\partial\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)}{\partial t}=\left[\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}-V(\mathbf{x})-\delta-(\beta_{11}|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{12}|\phi_{2}|^{2})\right]\phi_{1}-\lambda\phi_{2}+\mu_{\Phi}(t)\phi_{1},\\
&\qquad\frac{\partial\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)}{\partial t}=\left[\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}-V(\mathbf{x})-(\beta_{12}|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{22}|\phi_{2}|^{2})\right]\phi_{2}-\lambda\phi_{1}+\mu_{\Phi}(t)\phi_{2},\end{split}$ (9.31) where $\Phi(\mathbf{x},t)=(\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t),\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t))^{T}$ and $\mu_{\Phi}(t)$ is chosen such that the above CNGF is mass or
normalization conservative and it is given as $\displaystyle\mu_{\Phi}(t)$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|\Phi(\cdot,t)\|_{2}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\left(|\nabla\phi_{1}|^{2}+|\nabla\phi_{2}|^{2}\right)+V(\mathbf{x})(|\phi_{1}|^{2}+|\phi_{2}|^{2})+\delta|\phi_{1}|^{2}$ (9.32) $\displaystyle\qquad+\beta_{11}|\phi_{1}|^{4}+\beta_{22}|\phi_{2}|^{4}+2\beta_{12}|\phi_{1}|^{2}|\phi_{2}|^{2}+2\lambda\ \text{Re}(\phi_{1}{\overline{\phi}}_{2})\biggr]\,d\mathbf{x}$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle\frac{\mu(\Phi(\cdot,t))}{\|\Phi(\cdot,t)\|_{2}^{2}},\qquad t\geq 0.$ For the above CNGF, we have [ 19 ] Theorem 9.4 . For any given initial data $\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{x},0)=(\phi_{1}^{0}(\mathbf{x}),\phi_{2}^{0}(\mathbf{x}))^{T}:=\Phi^{(0)}(\mathbf{x}),\qquad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},$ (9.33) satisfying $\|\Phi^{(0)}\|_{2}^{2}=1$ , the CNGF
( 9.31 ) is mass or normalization conservative and energy
diminishing, i.e. $\|\Phi(\cdot,t)\|_{2}^{2}\equiv\|\Phi^{(0)}\|_{2}^{2}=1,\qquad E(\Phi(\cdot,t))\leq E(\Phi(\cdot,s)),\qquad 0\leq s\leq t.$ (9.34) For practical computation, here we also present a second-order in
both space and time full discretization for the above CNGF
( 9.31 ). For simplicity of notation, we introduce the method
for the case of one spatial dimension (1D) in a bounded domain $U=(a,b)$ with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition $\displaystyle\Phi(a,t)=\Phi(b,t)={\bf 0},\qquad t\geq 0.$ (9.35) Generalizations to higher dimensions are straightforward for tensor
product grids. Let $\Phi_{j}^{n}=(\phi_{1,j}^{n},\phi_{2,j}^{n})^{T}$ be the numerical
approximation of $\Phi(x_{j},t_{n})$ and $\Phi^{n}$ be the solution vector
with component $\Phi_{j}^{n}$ . In addition, denote $\Phi_{j}^{n+1/2}=(\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2},\phi_{2,j}^{n+1/2})^{T}$ with $\displaystyle\phi_{l,j}^{n+1/2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\phi_{l,j}^{n+1}+\phi_{l,j}^{n}\right),\qquad j=0,1,2,\ldots,M,\qquad l=1,2.$ (9.36) Then a second-order full
discretization for the CNGF ( 9.31 ) is given, for $j=1,2,\ldots,M-1$ and $n\geq 0$ , as $\displaystyle\frac{\phi_{1,j}^{n+1}-\phi_{1,j}^{n}}{\tau}=$ $\displaystyle\frac{\phi_{1,j+1}^{n+1/2}-2\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2}+\phi_{1,j-1}^{n+1/2}}{2h^{2}}-\left[V(x_{j})+\delta-\mu_{\Phi,h}^{n+1/2}\right]\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2}-\lambda\phi_{2,j}^{n+1/2}$ $\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\beta_{11}\left(|\phi_{1,j}^{n+1}|^{2}+|\phi_{1,j}^{n}|^{2}\right)+\beta_{12}\left(|\phi_{2,j}^{n+1}|^{2}+|\phi_{2,j}^{n}|^{2}\right)\right]\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2},$ $\displaystyle\frac{\phi_{2,j}^{n+1}-\phi_{2,j}^{n}}{\tau}=$ $\displaystyle\frac{\phi_{2,j+1}^{n+1/2}-2\phi_{2,j}^{n+1/2}+\phi_{2,j-1}^{n+1/2}}{2h^{2}}-\left[V(x_{j})-\mu_{\Phi,h}^{n+1/2}\right]\phi_{2,j}^{n+1/2}-\lambda\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2}$ $\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\beta_{12}\left(|\phi_{1,j}^{n+1}|^{2}+|\phi_{1,j}^{n}|^{2}\right)+\beta_{22}\left(|\phi_{2,j}^{n+1}|^{2}+|\phi_{2,j}^{n}|^{2}\right)\right]\phi_{2,j}^{n+1/2},$ where $\displaystyle\mu_{\Phi,h}^{n+1/2}=\frac{D_{\Phi,h}^{n+1/2}}{h\sum\limits_{j=0}^{M-1}\left(|\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2}|^{2}+|\phi_{2,j}^{n+1/2}|^{2}\right)},\qquad n\geq 0,$ (9.37) with $\displaystyle D_{\Phi,h}^{n+1/2}$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle h\sum\limits_{j=0}^{M-1}\biggl\{\sum\limits_{l=1}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2h^{2}}|\phi_{l,j+1}^{n+1/2}-\phi_{l,j}^{n+1/2}|^{2}+V(x_{j})|\phi_{l,j}^{n+1/2}|^{2}\right)+\delta|\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2}|^{2}$ (9.38) $\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{11}(|\phi_{1,j}^{n+1}|^{2}|+|\phi_{1,j}^{n}|^{2})|\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{22}(|\phi_{2,j}^{n+1}|^{2}+|\phi_{2,j}^{n}|^{2})|\phi_{2,j}^{n+1/2}|^{2}$ $\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{12}\left[(|\phi_{2,j}^{n+1}|^{2}+|\phi_{2,j}^{n}|^{2})|\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2}|^{2}+(|\phi_{1,j}^{n+1}|^{2}|+|\phi_{1,j}^{n}|^{2})|\phi_{2,j}^{n+1/2}|^{2}\right]$ $\displaystyle+2\lambda\ \text{Re}\left(\phi_{1,j}^{n+1/2}{\overline{\phi}}_{2,j}^{n+1/2}\right)\biggl\}.$ The boundary condition ( 9.35 ) is discretized as $\displaystyle\phi_{1,0}^{n+1}=\phi_{1,M}^{n+1}=\phi_{2,0}^{n+1}=\phi_{2,M}^{n+1}=0,\quad n=0,1,2,\ldots.$ (9.39) The initial data ( 9.33 ) is discretized as $\displaystyle\phi_{1,j}^{0}=\phi_{1}^{0}(x_{j}),\qquad\phi_{2,j}^{0}=\phi_{2}^{0}(x_{j}),\qquad j=0,1,\ldots,M.$ (9.40) In the above full discretization, at every time step, we need to
solve a fully nonlinear system which is very tedious in practical
computation. Below we present a more efficient discretization for
the CNGF ( 9.31 ) for computing the ground states. 9.5.2. Gradient flow with discrete normalization Another more efficient way to discretize the CNGF ( 9.31 ) is
through the construction of the following GFDN [ 19 ] : $\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial\phi_{1}}{\partial t}=\left[\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}-V(\mathbf{x})-\delta-(\beta_{11}|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{12}|\phi_{2}|^{2})\right]\phi_{1}-\lambda\phi_{2},\qquad\qquad\quad\\
\frac{\partial\phi_{2}}{\partial t}=\left[\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}-V(\mathbf{x})-(\beta_{12}|\phi_{1}|^{2}+\beta_{22}|\phi_{2}|^{2})\right]\phi_{2}-\lambda\phi_{1},\quad t\in(t_{n},t_{n+1}),\end{split}$ (9.41) followed by a
projection step as $\displaystyle\phi_{l}(\mathbf{x},t_{n+1}):=\phi_{l}(\mathbf{x},t_{n+1}^{+})=\sigma_{l}^{n+1}\;\phi_{l}(\mathbf{x}\,,t_{n+1}^{-}),\qquad l=1,2,\quad n\geq 0,$ (9.42) where $\phi_{l}(\mathbf{x},t_{n+1}^{\pm})=\lim\limits_{t\to t_{n+1}^{\pm}}\phi_{l}(\mathbf{x},t)$ ( $l=1,2$ ) and $\sigma_{l}^{n+1}$ ( $l=1,2$ )
are chosen such that $\displaystyle\|\Phi(\mathbf{x},t_{n+1})\|^{2}=\|\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t_{n+1})\|_{2}^{2}+\|\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t_{n+1})\|_{2}^{2}=1,\qquad n\geq 0.$ (9.43) The above GFDN ( 9.41 )-( 9.42 ) can be viewed as
applying the first-order splitting method to the CNGF ( 9.31 )
and the projection step ( 9.42 ) is equivalent to solving the
following ordinary differential equations (ODEs) $\displaystyle\frac{\partial\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)}{\partial t}=\mu_{\Phi}(t)\phi_{1},\qquad\frac{\partial\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)}{\partial t}=\mu_{\Phi}(t)\phi_{2},\qquad\quad t_{n}\leq t\leq t_{n+1},$ (9.44) which immediately suggests that the
projection constants in ( 9.42 ) are chosen as $\displaystyle\sigma_{1}^{n+1}=\sigma_{2}^{n+1},\qquad n\geq 0.$ (9.45) Plugging
( 9.45 ) and ( 9.42 ) into ( 9.43 ), we obtain $\displaystyle\sigma_{1}^{n+1}=\sigma_{2}^{n+1}=\frac{1}{\|\Phi(\cdot,t_{n+1}^{-})\|_{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\|\phi_{1}(\cdot,t_{n+1}^{-})\|_{2}^{2}+\|\phi_{2}(\cdot,t_{n+1}^{-})\|_{2}^{2}}},\;n\geq 0.$ (9.46) Then, BEFD in section 3.2 can be used to discretize the GFDN ( 9.41 )-( 9.42 ) and we omit the detailed scheme here, as the generalization is straightforward. 9.6. Numerical methods for computing dynamics To compute dynamics of a two component BEC, finite difference time domain methods in section 4.2 can be directly extended to solve the CGPEs ( 9.3 ). Here we focus on the time splitting methods.
For $n=0,1,\dots$ , from time $t=t_{n}=n\tau$ to $t=t_{n+1}=t_{n}+\tau$ , the CGPEs ( 9.3 )
are solved in three splitting steps [ 195 , 181 , 183 ] . One first
solves $\displaystyle i\frac{\partial\psi_{j}}{\partial t}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\psi_{j},\qquad j=1,2,$ (9.47) for the time step of length $\tau$ , followed by solving $\displaystyle i\frac{\partial\psi_{j}}{\partial t}=V_{j}({\mathbf{x}})\psi_{j}+\sum_{l=1}^{2}\beta_{jl}|\psi_{l}|^{2}\psi_{j},\qquad j=1,2,$ (9.48) for the same time step with $V_{1}(\mathbf{x})=V(\mathbf{x})+\delta$ and $V_{2}(\mathbf{x})=V(\mathbf{x})$ , and then by solving $\displaystyle i\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial t}=-\lambda\psi_{2},\quad i\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial t}=-\lambda\psi_{1},$ (9.49) for the same time step. For time $t\in[t_{n},t_{n+1}]$ ,
the ODE system ( 9.48 ) leaves $|\psi_{1}({\mathbf{x}},t)|$ and $|\psi_{2}({\mathbf{x}},t)|$ invariant in $t$ , and thus it
can be integrated exactly to obtain [ 33 , 31 , 47 , 48 , 193 ] , for $j=1,2$ and $t\in[t_{n},t_{n+1}]$ $\displaystyle\psi_{j}({\mathbf{x}},t)=\psi_{j}(\mathbf{x},t_{n})\exp\left[-i\left(V_{j}({\mathbf{x}})+\sum_{l=1}^{2}\beta_{jl}\left|\psi_{l}(\mathbf{x},t_{n})\right|^{2}\right)(t-t_{n})\right].$ (9.50) For the ODE system ( 9.49 ), we can rewrite it as $\displaystyle i\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t}=-\lambda A\Psi,\qquad\mathrm{with}\quad A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\quad{\mathrm{a}nd}\quad\Psi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{1}\\
\psi_{2}\end{array}\right).$ Since $A$ is a real and symmetric matrix, it can be
diagonalized and integrated exactly , and then we obtain [ 15 , 195 ] , for $t\in[t_{n},t_{n+1}]$ $\Psi(\mathbf{x},t)=e^{i\lambda A\;(t-t_{n})}\Psi(\mathbf{x},t_{n})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\cos\left(\lambda(t-t_{n})\right)&i\sin\left(\lambda(t-t_{n})\right)\\
i\sin\left(\lambda(t-t_{n})\right)&\cos\left(\lambda(t-t_{n})\right)\\
\end{array}\right)\Psi(\mathbf{x},t_{n}).$ Then, time splitting spectral method introduced in sections 4 and 6 can be applied to compute the dynamics of the CGPEs ( 9.3 ), by a suitable composition of the above three steps (cf. section 4.1 ). The detailed scheme is omitted here for brevity. 9.7. Numerical results In this section, we will report
the ground states of ( 9.15 ), computed by our numerical methods. Figure 9.1. Ground states $\Phi_{g}=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})^{T}$ in Example 9.1 when $\delta=0$ and $\lambda=-1$ for different $\beta$ . Figure 9.2. Mass of each component $N(\phi_{j})=\|\phi_{j}\|^{2}$ ( $j=1,2$ ),
energy $E:=E(\Phi_{g})$ and chemical potential $\mu:=\mu(\Phi_{g})$ of the ground states in Example 9.1 when $\beta=100$ and $\delta=0,1$ for different $\lambda$ . Figure 9.3. Mass of each component $N(\phi_{j})=\|\phi_{j}\|^{2}$ ( $j=1,2$ ),
energy $E:=E(\Phi_{g})$ and chemical potential $\mu:=\mu(\Phi_{g})$ of the ground states in Example 9.1 when $\beta=100$ and $\lambda=0,-5$ for different $\delta$ . Example 9.1 . Ground states of a two-component BEC with an external
driving field when $B$ is positive definite, i.e. we take $d=1$ , $V(x)=\frac{1}{2}x^{2}$ and $\beta_{11}:\beta_{12}:\beta_{22}=(1:0.94:0.97)\beta$ in
( 9.15 ) [ 15 , 19 ] . In this case, since $\lambda\leq 0$ and $B$ is positive definite when $\beta>0$ , thus we know
that the positive ground state $\Phi_{g}=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})^{T}$ is
unique. In our computations, we take the computational domain $U=[-16,16]$ with mesh size $h=\frac{1}{32}$ and time step $\tau=0.1$ . The initial data in ( 9.33 ) is chosen as $\displaystyle\phi_{1}^{0}(x)=\phi_{2}^{0}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi^{1/4}\sqrt{2}}e^{-x^{2}/2},\qquad x\in{\mathbb{R}}.$ (9.56) Fig. 9.1 plots the ground states $\Phi_{g}$ when $\delta=0$ and $\lambda=-1$ for different $\beta$ . Fig. 9.2 shows mass of each component $N(\phi_{j})=\|\phi_{j}\|^{2}$ ( $j=1,2$ ),
energy $E:=E(\Phi_{g})$ and chemical potential $\mu:=\mu(\Phi_{g})$ of the ground states
when $\beta=100$ and $\delta=0,1$ for different $\lambda$ , and
Fig. 9.3 depicts similar results when $\beta=100$ and $\lambda=0,-5$ for
different $\delta$ . 10. Perspectives and challenges So far, we have introduced mathematical results and numerical methods for ground states and dynamics of a single/two component rotating/nonrotating BEC with/without dipole-dipole interactions described by mean field GPE. Despite these BEC systems, much progress has been made towards realizing other kinds of gaseous BEC, such as spinor condensates, condensates at finite temperature, Bose-Fermi mixtures, etc. These achievements have brought great challenges to atomic physics community and scientific computing community for modeling, simulating and understanding various interesting phenomenons. 10.1. Spin-1 BEC In earlier BEC experiments, the atoms were confined in magnetic trap [ 12 , 86 , 59 ] ,
in which the spin degrees of freedom is frozen. In recent years, experimental
achievement of spin-1 and spin-2
condensates [ 50 , 124 ] offers new
regimes to study various quantum phenomena that are generally absent in
a single component condensate.
The spinor condensate is achieved experimentally when an optical trap,
instead of a magnetic trap,
is used to provide equal confinement for all hyperfine states. The theoretical studies of spinor condensate have been carried out
in several papers since the achievement of it in experiments [ 115 , 128 ] . In contrast to single component
condensate, a spin- $F$ ( $F\in{\mathbb{N}}$ ) condensate is described
by a generalized coupled GPEs which consists of $2F+1$ equations,
each governing one of the $2F+1$ hyperfine states $(m_{F}=-F,-F+1,...,F-1,F)$ within the mean-field approximation. For a
spin-1 condensate, at temperature much lower than the critical
temperature $T_{c}$ , the three-components wave function $\Psi:=\Psi(\mathbf{x},t)=(\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t),\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x},t),\psi_{-1}(\mathbf{x},t))^{T}$ are well
described by the following coupled GPEs [ 124 , 36 ] , $\displaystyle i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)=$ $\displaystyle\left[-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+(c_{0}+c_{2})\left(|\psi_{1}|^{2}+|\psi_{0}|^{2}\right)+(c_{0}-c_{2})|\psi_{-1}|^{2}\right]\psi_{1}$ $\displaystyle+c_{2}\,{\overline{\psi}}_{-1}\,\psi_{0}^{2},$ (10.1) $\displaystyle i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x},t)=$ $\displaystyle\left[-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+(c_{0}+c_{2})\left(|\psi_{1}|^{2}+|\psi_{-1}|^{2}\right)+c_{0}|\psi_{0}|^{2}\right]\psi_{0}$ $\displaystyle+2c_{2}\,\psi_{-1}\,{\overline{\psi}}_{0}\,\psi_{1},$ (10.2) $\displaystyle i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\psi_{-1}(\mathbf{x},t)=$ $\displaystyle\left[-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\nabla^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})+(c_{0}+c_{2})\left(|\psi_{-1}|^{2}+|\psi_{0}|^{2}\right)+(c_{0}-c_{2})|\psi_{1}|^{2}\right]\psi_{-1}$ $\displaystyle+c_{2}\,\psi_{0}^{2}\,{\overline{\psi}}_{1},\qquad\mathbf{x}=(x,y,z)^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.$ (10.3) Here $V(\mathbf{x})$ is an external trapping potential. There are two atomic collision terms, $c_{0}=\frac{4\pi\hbar^{2}}{3m}(a_{0}+2a_{2})$ and $c_{2}=\frac{4\pi\hbar^{2}}{3m}(a_{2}-a_{0})$ , expressed in terms of the $s$ -wave scattering lengths, $a_{0}$ and $a_{2}$ , for scattering
channel of total hyperfine spin 0 (anti-parallel spin collision)
and spin 2 (parallel spin collision), respectively. The usual
mean-field interaction, $c_{0}$ , is positive for repulsive
interaction and negative for attractive interaction. The
spin-exchange interaction, $c_{2}$ , is positive for
antiferromagnetic interaction and negative for ferromagnetic
interaction. The wave function is normalized according to $\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{2}^{2}:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|\Psi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}\;d\mathbf{x}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\sum_{l=-1}^{1}|\psi_{l}(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}\;d\mathbf{x}:=\sum_{l=-1}^{1}\|\psi_{l}\|_{2}^{2}=N,$ (10.4) where $N$ is the total number
of particles in the condensate. This normalization is conserved by coupled GPEs ( 10.1 )-( 10.3 ), and so are the magnetization $\displaystyle M(\Psi(\cdot,t)):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\left[|\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}-|\psi_{-1}(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}\right]d\mathbf{x}\equiv M(\Psi(\cdot,0))=M$ (10.5) and the energy per particle $\displaystyle E(\Psi(\cdot,t))$ $\displaystyle=$ $\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\biggl\{\sum_{l=-1}^{1}\left(\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}|\nabla\psi_{l}|^{2}+V(\mathbf{x})|\psi_{l}|^{2}\right)+(c_{0}-c_{2})|\psi_{1}|^{2}|\psi_{-1}|^{2}$ (10.6) $\displaystyle+\frac{c_{0}}{2}|\psi_{0}|^{4}+\frac{c_{0}+c_{2}}{2}\Bigl[|\psi_{1}|^{4}+|\psi_{-1}|^{4}+2|\psi_{0}|^{2}\left(|\psi_{1}|^{2}+|\psi_{-1}|^{2}\right)\Bigr]$ $\displaystyle+c_{2}\left({\overline{\psi}}_{-1}\psi_{0}^{2}{\overline{\psi}}_{1}+\psi_{-1}{\overline{\psi}}_{0}^{2}\psi_{1}\right)\biggr\}\;d\mathbf{x}\equiv E(\Psi(\cdot,0)),\qquad t\geq 0.$ Then ground states of spin-1 BEC can be defined as the minimizer of energy $E$ under the normalization and magnetization constraints [ 36 , 45 , 26 ] . In particular, when the external traps for all the components are the same,
ground states for ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic spin-1 BECs can be simplified [ 26 ] . Generally speaking, for spin- $F$ BEC, the complicated nonlinear terms in ( 10.1 )-( 10.3 ) lead to new difficulties for mathematical analysis and numerical simulation [ 182 ] . Much work needs to be done in future, especially when rotational frame and dipole-dipole interactions are taken into account in spin- $F$ BECs [ 124 ] . 10.2. Bogoliubov excitation The theory of interacting Bose gases, developed by Bogoliubov in 1947, is very useful and important to understand BEC in dilute atomic gases. One of the key issue is the Bogoliubov excitation. To describe the condensate, we have the lowest order approximation, i.e., the Gross-Pitaevskii energy by assuming that all particles are in the ground state. However, due to the interactions between the atoms, there is a small portion occupying the excited states. Thus, if a higher order approximation of the ground state energy is considered, excitations have to be included. Using a perturbation technique, Bogoliubov has investigated this problem and shown that the excited states of a system of interacting Bose particles can be described by a system of noninteracting quasi-particles satisfying the Bogoliubov dispersion relation. To determine the Bogoliubov excitation spectrum, we consider small perturbations
around the ground state of Eq. ( 1.19 ). For simplicity we
assume a vanishing harmonic potential $V(\mathbf{x})=0$ and
homogeneous density $\nu$ . A stationary state of the
GPE ( 1.19 ) is given by $\psi(\mathbf{x},t)=\psi(t)=e^{-i\mu t}\sqrt{\nu}$ with the chemical potential $\mu=\beta\nu.$ (10.7) Now we add a local perturbation $\xi(\mathbf{x},t)$ to the
stationary state $\psi(t)$ , that is, $\psi(\mathbf{x},t)=e^{-i\mu t}[\sqrt{\nu}+\xi(\mathbf{x},t)]$ . We expand the
perturbation in a plane wave basis as $\xi(\mathbf{x},t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigl(u_{{\bf q}}e^{i({\bf q}\cdot\mathbf{x}-\omega_{{\bf q}}t)}+\overline{v_{{\bf q}}}e^{-i({\bf q}\cdot\mathbf{x}-\omega_{{\bf q}}t)}\bigr)d{\bf q}$ and insert $\psi(\mathbf{x},t)$ into
Eq. ( 1.19 ). Here, $\omega_{{\bf q}}$ are the excitation
frequencies of quasimomentum ${\bf q}$ and $u_{{\bf q}}$ , $v_{{\bf q}}$ are the mode functions. Keeping terms
linear in the excitations $u_{{\bf q}}$ and $v_{{\bf q}}$ we find
the Bogoliubov-de Gennes equations $\begin{split}\omega_{{\bf q}}u_{{\bf q}}&=\frac{{\bf q}^{2}}{2}u_{{\bf q}}+\nu\beta(v_{{\bf q}}+u_{{\bf q}}),\\
-\omega_{{\bf q}}v_{{\bf q}}&=\frac{{\bf q}^{2}}{2}v_{{\bf q}}+\nu\beta(v_{{\bf q}}+u_{{\bf q}}).\end{split}$ (10.8) Then we can find the eigenenergies of
Eq. ( 10.8 ) by solving the eigenvalue problem. The resulting Bogoliubov
energy $E_{B}({\bf q})=\omega_{{\bf q}}$ is determined by $E_{B}^{2}({\bf q})=\frac{{\bf q}^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{{\bf q}^{2}}{2}+2\beta\nu\right).$ (10.9) When an external potential is considered, the Bogoliubov energy would be more complicated.
In 1999, the Bogoliubov excitation spectrum was observed for the first time in atomic BEC [ 170 ] , using light scattering. Later in 2008, observation of Bogoliubov excitations was announced in
exciton-polariton condensates [ 179 ] . Such elementary excitations are crucial in understanding various phenomenon in BEC. 10.3. BEC at finite temperature The process of creating a BEC in a trap by means of evaporative cooling starts in a
regime covered by the quantum Boltzmann equation (QBE) and finishes in a regime where the GPE is expected to be valid. The GPE is capable to describe the
main properties of the condensate at very low temperatures, it treats the condensate
as a classical field and neglects quantum and thermal fluctuations. As
a consequence, the theory breaks down at higher temperatures where the non-condensed fraction of the gas cloud is significant. An approach which allows
the treatment of both condensate and noncondensate parts simultaneously
was developed in [ 190 , 39 ] . The resulting equations of motion reduce to a generalized GPE for the
condensate wave function coupled with a semiclassical QBE for the thermal
cloud: $\begin{split}&i\hbar\partial_{t}\psi(\mathbf{x},t)=\left[-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\nabla^{2}+(n_{c}(\mathbf{x},t)+2n(\mathbf{x},t))g-iR(\mathbf{x},t)\right]\psi,\\
&\frac{\partial F}{\partial t}+\frac{{\bf p}}{m}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}F-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}U\cdot\nabla_{{\bf p}}F=Q(F)+Q_{c}(F),\end{split}$ (10.10) where $n_{c}(\mathbf{x},t)=|\psi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}$ is the condensate density, $F:=F(\mathbf{x},{\bf p},t)$ describes the distribution of thermal atoms in the phase space and it gives the particle number with momentum ${\bf p}$ at position $\mathbf{x}$ and time $t$ in the thermal cloud. $n(\mathbf{x},t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F(\mathbf{x},{\bf p},t)/(2\pi\hbar)^{3}d{\bf p}$ , $V(\mathbf{x})$ is the confining potential and $g=4\pi\hbar^{2}a_{s}/m$ . The collision integral $Q(F)$ is given by $\displaystyle Q(F)=$ $\displaystyle\frac{2g^{2}}{(2\pi)^{5}\hbar^{7}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\delta({\bf p}+{\bf p}_{\ast}-{\bf p}^{\prime}-{\bf p}_{\ast}^{\prime})\times\delta(\epsilon+\epsilon_{\ast}-\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon_{\ast}^{\prime})$ $\displaystyle\times[(1+F)(1+F_{\ast})F^{\prime}F^{\prime}_{\ast}-FF_{\ast}(1+F^{\prime})(1+F^{\prime}_{\ast})]\,d\,{\bf p}_{\ast}\,d{\bf p}^{\prime}\,d{\bf p}_{\ast}^{\prime},$ where $\epsilon=U(\mathbf{x},t)+{\bf p}^{2}/2m$ , $U(\mathbf{x},t)=V(\mathbf{x})+2gn_{c}(\mathbf{x},t)+2gn(\mathbf{x},t)$ and $\delta(\cdot)$ is the Dirac distribution. $Q_{c}(F)$ which describes collisions between condensate and non condensate particles
is given by $\displaystyle Q_{c}(F)=$ $\displaystyle\frac{2g^{2}n_{c}}{(2\pi)^{2}\hbar^{4}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\delta(m{\bf v}_{c}+{\bf p}_{\ast}-{\bf p}^{\prime}-{\bf p}_{\ast}^{\prime})$ $\displaystyle\times\delta(\epsilon_{c}+\epsilon_{\ast}-\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon_{\ast}^{\prime})[\delta({\bf p}-{\bf p}_{\ast})-\delta({\bf p}-{\bf p}^{\prime})-\delta({\bf p}-{\bf p}_{\ast}^{\prime})]$ $\displaystyle\times[(1+F_{\ast})F^{\prime}F^{\prime}_{\ast}-F_{\ast}(1+F^{\prime})(1+F^{\prime}_{\ast})]\,d\,{\bf p}_{\ast}\,d{\bf p}^{\prime}\,d{\bf p}_{\ast}^{\prime},$ where $\displaystyle\epsilon_{c}(\mathbf{x},t)=\frac{1}{2}m{\bf v}_{c}(\mathbf{x},t)^{2}+\mu_{c}(\mathbf{x},t),$ (10.11) and ${\bf v}_{c}$ is the quantum hydrodynamic velocity, $\mu_{c}$ is the effective potential acting on the condensate [ 190 , 108 ] . $R(\mathbf{x},t)$ is then written as $\displaystyle R(\mathbf{x},t)=\frac{\hbar}{2n_{c}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{Q_{c}(F)}{(2\pi\hbar)^{3}}\,d{\bf p}.$ (10.12) Note that for low temperatures $T\to 0$ we have $n,R\to 0$ and we recover the
conventional GPE. The system ( 10.10 ) is normalized as $N_{c}(0)=N_{c}^{0}$ and $N_{t}(0)=N_{t}^{0}$ with $\displaystyle N_{c}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\psi(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x},\quad N_{t}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|n(\mathbf{x},t)|^{2}d\,\mathbf{x},\quad t\geq 0,$ (10.13) where $N_{c}^{0}$ and $N_{t}^{0}$ are the number of particles in the condensate and thermal
cloud at time $t=0$ , respectively. It is easy to see from the equations ( 10.10 ) that the total number of particles defined as $N_{\rm total}(t)=N_{c}(t)+N_{t}(t)=N^{0}_{\rm total}=N^{0}_{c}+N^{0}_{t}$ is conserved. For this set of equations, the GPE part can be solved efficiently, and the main trouble comes from the Boltzmann equation part. Alternatively, projected GPE model is also used for simulating BEC at finite temperature [ 85 , 87 ] .
